\section*{215. A Supplementary Memoir on the Problem of Disturbed Elliptic Motion}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{215. A Supplementary Memoir on the Problem of Disturbed Elliptic Motion}

\begin{center}
  [From the \textit{Memoirs of the Royal Astronomical Society}, vol.~xxix. (1861), pp.~191--306.\\
  Read January 11, 1861.]
\end{center}

\vspace{1em}

\noindent\textbf{[Continued from page 350.]}

\vspace{0.5em}

% ============================================================
\subsection*{Page 351}
% ============================================================

I write $r^{2}\dfrac{dp}{dt} = h$. We thus have
\[
\frac{d}{dt}\frac{dr}{dt} - r\left(\frac{dp}{dt}\right)^{2} = \frac{n^{2}a^{3}}{r^{2}} + \frac{d\Omega}{dr},
\]
\[
r^{2}\frac{dp}{dt} = h,
\]
and
\[
dh = \frac{d\Omega}{dp}\,dt;
\]
the remaining equations are
\begin{align*}
d\Sigma - \cos\Phi\,d\Theta &= -\sin\Phi\,[\cos(\Theta - \sigma')\sin\phi'\,d\theta' - \sin(\Theta - \sigma')\,d\phi'],\\
\cos(p - \Sigma)\sin\Phi\,d\Theta - \sin(p - \Sigma)\,d\Phi &=
\sin(p - \Sigma)\,[\sin(\Theta - \sigma')\sin\phi'\,d\theta' + \cos(\Theta - \sigma')\,d\phi'] \\
&\quad - \cos(p - \Sigma)\cos\Phi\,[\cos(\Theta - \sigma')\sin\phi'\,d\theta' - \sin(\Theta - \sigma')\,d\phi'],\\
\sin(p - \Sigma)\sin\Phi\,d\Theta + \cos(p - \Sigma)\,d\Phi &=
\frac{1}{h}\frac{d\Omega}{dy}\,dt \\
&\quad - \cos(p - \Sigma)\,[\sin(\Theta - \sigma')\sin\phi'\,d\theta' + \cos(\Theta - \sigma')\,d\phi'] \\
&\quad - \sin(p - \Sigma)\cos\Phi\,[\cos(\Theta - \sigma')\sin\phi'\,d\theta' - \sin(\Theta - \sigma')\,d\phi'].
\end{align*}
Moreover we have
\begin{align*}
\cos(p - \Sigma)\frac{d\Omega}{dy} &= -\cot\phi\frac{d\Omega}{d\Sigma} - \csc\Phi\frac{d\Omega}{d\Theta},\\
\sin(p - \Sigma)\frac{d\Omega}{dy} &= \qquad\qquad \frac{d\Omega}{d\Phi},
\end{align*}
by means of which the preceding two equations are reduced to
\begin{align*}
d\Theta &= \frac{\csc\Phi}{h}\frac{d\Omega}{d\Phi}\,dt \\
&\quad - \cot\Phi\,[\cos(\Theta - \sigma')\sin\phi'\,d\theta' - \sin(\Theta - \sigma')\,d\phi'],\\
d\Phi &= -\frac{\cot\Phi}{h}\frac{d\Omega}{d\Sigma}\,dt - \frac{\csc\Phi}{h}\frac{d\Omega}{d\Theta}\,dt \\
&\quad - \qquad [\sin(\Theta - \sigma')\sin\phi'\,d\theta' + \cos(\Theta - \sigma')\,d\phi'];
\end{align*}
and we then have
\begin{align*}
d\Sigma &= \frac{\cot\Phi}{h}\frac{d\Omega}{d\Phi}\,dt \\
&\quad - \csc\Phi\,[\cos(\Theta - \sigma')\sin\phi'\,d\theta' - \sin(\Theta - \sigma')\,d\phi'].
\end{align*}

% ============================================================
\newpage
\subsection*{Page 352}
% ============================================================

The entire system consequently is
\[
\frac{dr}{dt},\quad
\frac{dh}{dt},\quad
\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{dr}{dt}\right),\quad
\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{dp}{dt}\right),\quad
r^{2}\frac{dr}{dt},
\]
and
\[
\frac{dh}{dt} = \frac{d\Omega}{dp},
\]
to which are to be added the equations for $d\Theta$, $d\Phi$, $d\Sigma$ given above.

\vspace{0.5em}

To obtain the formulae of my Memoir before referred to, it would only be necessary to write $h = na^{2}\sqrt{1-e^{2}}$, and complete the solution by applying to the first equation the method of the variation of the elements; but I prefer leaving the system in its present form, as it is one which may perhaps be useful.

\vspace{0.5em}

The formulae have especial reference to the lunar theory, and the orbit $xyz$ denotes the fixed ecliptic, $xy_{1}z_{1}$ the varying ecliptic, and $xy_{2}z_{2}$ the instantaneous orbit of the moon. But if, instead of considering with Hansen the instantaneous orbit of the moon, the position of the moon is determined (as in the theories of Clairaut, Plana, and others) by

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=3em]
  \item $r$, the radius vector,
  \item $v$, the longitude,
  \item $y$, the latitude,
\end{itemize}

where the longitude is measured on the variable ecliptic, then if, in the general expressions for $P$, $Q$, $R$, we first neglect the terms involving $d\theta'$, $d\phi'$, $d\psi'$, and then write $\theta$, $\sigma'$, $\phi'$, $\Theta$, $\Phi$, $\Psi$, $\nu$, $\gamma$ in the place of $\Theta$, $\Sigma$, $\Phi$, we find
\begin{align*}
P\,dt &= \sin\gamma\,dv \\
&\quad + \cos\gamma\,[\sin(v - \sigma')\sin\phi'\,d\theta' + \cos(v - \sigma')\,d\phi'] \\
&\quad + \sin\gamma\,[-d\psi' + \cos\phi'\,d\theta'],
\end{align*}

% ============================================================
\newpage
\subsection*{Page 353}
% ============================================================

\begin{align*}
R\,dt &= \cos\gamma\,dv \\
&\quad - \sin\gamma\,[\sin(v - \sigma')\sin\phi'\,d\theta' + \cos(v - \sigma')\,d\phi'] \\
&\quad + \cos\gamma\,[-d\psi' + \cos\phi'\,d\theta'],
\end{align*}
and with these values the expression for the \textit{Vis viva} function is, as before,
\[
T\,dt = \tfrac{1}{2}\{dr^{2} + r^{2}(Q^{2} + R^{2})\,dv\}.
\]

The longitude may be measured from a departure-point, and then the expressions for $P$, $Q$, $R$, may be simplified by omitting the terms which contain $-d\psi' + \cos\phi'\,d\theta'$.

\vspace{1em}

\noindent\textbf{Addition.}

\vspace{0.5em}

I have in the course of the memoir used some properties connected with the theory of rotation, but it is proper to give the analytical investigation.

Suppose that the rectangular coordinates $(X, Y, Z)$ are connected with the rectangular coordinates $(X_{1}, Y_{1}, Z_{1})$ by the equations
\begin{align*}
X &= \alpha X_{1} + \beta Y_{1} + \gamma Z_{1},\\
Y &= \alpha' X_{1} + \beta' Y_{1} + \gamma' Z_{1},\\
Z &= \alpha'' X_{1} + \beta'' Y_{1} + \gamma'' Z_{1},
\end{align*}
then we have
\begin{align*}
dX &= \alpha\,dX_{1} + \beta\,dY_{1} + \gamma\,dZ_{1} + X_{1}\,d\alpha + Y_{1}\,d\beta + Z_{1}\,d\gamma,\\
dY &= \alpha'\,dX_{1} + \beta'\,dY_{1} + \gamma'\,dZ_{1} + X_{1}\,d\alpha' + Y_{1}\,d\beta' + Z_{1}\,d\gamma',\\
dZ &= \alpha''\,dX_{1} + \beta''\,dY_{1} + \gamma''\,dZ_{1} + X_{1}\,d\alpha'' + Y_{1}\,d\beta'' + Z_{1}\,d\gamma'',
\end{align*}
and then, putting
\begin{align*}
p\,dt &= \beta\,d\gamma + \beta'\,d\gamma' + \beta''\,d\gamma'',\\
q\,dt &= \gamma\,d\alpha + \gamma'\,d\alpha' + \gamma''\,d\alpha'',\\
r\,dt &= \alpha\,d\beta + \alpha'\,d\beta' + \alpha''\,d\beta'',
\end{align*}
where $p$, $q$, $r$ are the rotations round $X_{1}$, $Y_{1}$, $Z_{1}$, from $Y_{1}$ to $Z_{1}$, $Z_{1}$ to $X_{1}$, and $X_{1}$ to $Y_{1}$ respectively, we have
\begin{align*}
\alpha\,dX + \alpha'\,dY + \alpha''\,dZ &= dX_{1} + (-rY_{1} + qZ_{1})\,dt,\\
\beta\,dX + \beta'\,dY + \beta''\,dZ &= dY_{1} + (-pZ_{1} + rX_{1})\,dt,\\
\gamma\,dX + \gamma'\,dY + \gamma''\,dZ &= dZ_{1} + (-qX_{1} + pY_{1})\,dt,
\end{align*}
where, considering $XYZ$ as fixed axes, the left-hand sides are the displacements in the directions of the moveable axes $X_{1}Y_{1}Z_{1}$, arising from the variations of the coordinates $X, Y, Z$, and the variation of the position of these axes; and these displacements have therefore the values given by the right-hand sides. This is, of course, well known. It may be added that if the axes $XYZ$ are moveable, and $dX$, $dY$, $dZ$ denote the displacements in the directions of these axes, the formulae are still true.

% ============================================================
\newpage
\subsection*{Page 354}
% ============================================================

Suppose that $\Omega$ is a function of $X$, $Y$, $Z$; say $\Omega = \Omega(X, Y, Z)$, we have
\[
d\Omega = \frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial X}\,dX + \frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial Y}\,dY + \frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial Z}\,dZ,
\]
which may be written
\begin{align*}
d\Omega &= \left(\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial X}\alpha + \frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial Y}\alpha' + \frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial Z}\alpha''\right)(\alpha\,dX + \alpha'\,dY + \alpha''\,dZ) \\
&\quad + \left(\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial X}\beta + \frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial Y}\beta' + \frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial Z}\beta''\right)(\beta\,dX + \beta'\,dY + \beta''\,dZ) \\
&\quad + \left(\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial X}\gamma + \frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial Y}\gamma' + \frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial Z}\gamma''\right)(\gamma\,dX + \gamma'\,dY + \gamma''\,dZ),
\end{align*}
and the coefficients of transformation $\alpha$, $\beta$, $\gamma$, \&c., being independent of $X$, $Y$, $Z$ and $X_{1}$, $Y_{1}$, $Z_{1}$, the first factors are $\dfrac{\partial\Omega}{\partial X_{1}}$, $\dfrac{\partial\Omega}{\partial Y_{1}}$, $\dfrac{\partial\Omega}{\partial Z_{1}}$; and we have
\begin{align*}
d\Omega &= \frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial X_{1}}[dX_{1} + (-rY_{1} + qZ_{1})\,dt] \\
&\quad + \frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial Y_{1}}[dY_{1} + (-pZ_{1} + rX_{1})\,dt] \\
&\quad + \frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial Z_{1}}[dZ_{1} + (-qX_{1} + pY_{1})\,dt],
\end{align*}
which is a theorem assumed in the memoir.

The expressions of $P$, $Q$, $R$ in the memoir depend upon the composition of several rotations. If, in Lam\'e's very convenient notation, we write

\[
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
  & X_{1} & Y_{1} & Z_{1} \\ \hline
X & \alpha & \beta & \gamma \\
Y & \alpha' & \beta' & \gamma' \\
Z & \alpha'' & \beta'' & \gamma''
\end{array}
\]
to denote the before-mentioned relation between $(X, Y, Z)$ and $(X_{1}, Y_{1}, Z_{1})$, then the tables

\[
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
  & X & Y & Z \\ \hline
X_{1} & \alpha & \beta & \gamma \\
Y_{1} & \alpha' & \beta' & \gamma' \\
Z_{1} & \alpha'' & \beta'' & \gamma''
\end{array}
\quad
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
  & X_{1} & Y_{1} & Z_{1} \\ \hline
X_{1} & 1 & 0 & 0 \\
Y_{1} & 0 & 1 & 0 \\
Z_{1} & 0 & 0 & 1
\end{array}
\quad
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
  & X_{1} & Y_{1} & Z_{1} \\ \hline
X_{1} & \alpha & \beta & \gamma \\
Y_{1} & \alpha' & \beta' & \gamma' \\
Z_{1} & \alpha'' & \beta'' & \gamma''
\end{array}
\]
lead to the table

\[
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
  & X_{1} & Y_{1} & Z_{1} \\ \hline
X & (\alpha, \beta, \gamma)(A, A', A'') & (\alpha, \beta, \gamma)(B, B', B'') & (\alpha, \beta, \gamma)(C, C', C'') \\
Y & (\alpha', \beta', \gamma')(A, A', A'') & (\alpha', \beta', \gamma')(B, B', B'') & (\alpha', \beta', \gamma')(C, C', C'') \\
Z & (\alpha'', \beta'', \gamma'')(A, A', A'') & (\alpha'', \beta'', \gamma'')(B, B', B'') & (\alpha'', \beta'', \gamma'')(C, C', C'')
\end{array}
\]
where for shortness $(\alpha, \beta, \gamma)(A, A', A'')$, \&c.\ stand for $\alpha A + \beta A' + \gamma A''$, \&c.; these are of course the ordinary formulae for the composition of transformations from one set of rectangular axes to another. We may say that the coefficients of transformation from $(X, Y, Z)$ to $(X_{1}, Y_{1}, Z_{1})$ are $(\alpha, \beta, \gamma, \&c.)_{01}$. And in dealing with several sets of coordinates, the coefficients of transformation may be distinguished by suffixes.

% ============================================================
\newpage
\subsection*{Page 355}
% ============================================================

Thus I assume that the coefficients of transformation
\begin{align*}
\text{from }(x_{0}, y_{0}, z_{0})&\text{ to }(x_{1}, y_{1}, z_{1})\text{ are }(\alpha, \beta, \gamma, \&c.)_{01},\\
\text{from }(x_{1}, y_{1}, z_{1})&\text{ to }(x_{2}, y_{2}, z_{2})\text{ are }(\alpha, \beta, \gamma, \&c.)_{12},\\
\text{from }(x_{2}, y_{2}, z_{2})&\text{ to }(x_{3}, y_{3}, z_{3})\text{ are }(\alpha, \beta, \gamma, \&c.)_{23},
\end{align*}
where $(\alpha, \beta, \gamma, \&c.)_{02}$ can be obtained as above from $(\alpha, \beta, \gamma, \&c.)_{01}$ and $(\alpha, \beta, \gamma, \&c.)_{12}$.

The rotations $p$, $q$, $r$ may be distinguished by suffixes in like manner, viz.\ $(p, q, r)_{01}$ will denote the rotations from $(x_{0}, y_{0}, z_{0})$ to $(x_{1}, y_{1}, z_{1})$ and so on.

There is no difficulty in obtaining first
\begin{align*}
p_{02} &= p_{01} + (\alpha, \alpha', \alpha'')_{01}(p, q, r)_{12},\\
q_{02} &= q_{01} + (\beta, \beta', \beta'')_{01}(p, q, r)_{12},\\
r_{02} &= r_{01} + (\gamma, \gamma', \gamma'')_{01}(p, q, r)_{12},
\end{align*}
and then by a repetition of the like transformation
\begin{align*}
p_{03} &= p_{02} + (\alpha, \alpha', \alpha'')_{02}(p, q, r)_{23},\\
q_{03} &= q_{02} + (\beta, \beta', \beta'')_{02}(p, q, r)_{23},\\
r_{03} &= r_{02} + (\gamma, \gamma', \gamma'')_{02}(p, q, r)_{23},
\end{align*}
and in like manner for any number of systems, but for the present purpose this is enough, as $p_{03}$, $q_{03}$, $r_{03}$ are in fact the $P$, $Q$, $R$ of the memoir.

% ============================================================
\newpage
\subsection*{Page 356}
% ============================================================

And if $\theta$, $\sigma'$, $\phi'$, $\Theta$, $\Sigma$, $\Phi$, $\nu$, $\gamma$ signify as before, then the coefficients of transformation from $(x_{0}, y_{0}, z_{0})$ to $(x_{1}, y_{1}, z_{1})$ are given by the table
\[
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
  & x_{1} & y_{1} & z_{1} \\ \hline
x_{0} & \cos\sigma'\cos\theta' + \sin\sigma'\sin\theta'\cos\phi' & \sin\sigma'\cos\theta' - \cos\sigma'\sin\theta'\cos\phi' & \sin\theta'\sin\phi' \\
y_{0} & \cos\sigma'\sin\theta' - \sin\sigma'\cos\theta'\cos\phi' & \sin\sigma'\sin\theta' + \cos\sigma'\cos\theta'\cos\phi' & -\cos\theta'\sin\phi' \\
z_{0} & \sin\sigma'\sin\phi' & \cos\sigma'\sin\phi' & \cos\phi'
\end{array}
\]
and the corresponding rotation coefficients are
\begin{align*}
p_{01}\,dt &= -\sin\sigma'\sin\phi'\,d\theta' + \cos\sigma'\,d\phi',\\
q_{01}\,dt &= \cos\sigma'\sin\phi'\,d\theta' + \sin\sigma'\,d\phi',\\
r_{01}\,dt &= -d\sigma' + \cos\phi'\,d\theta'.
\end{align*}

The coefficients of transformation from $(x_{1}, y_{1}, z_{1})$ to $(x_{2}, y_{2}, z_{2})$ are given by the table
\[
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
  & x_{2} & y_{2} & z_{2} \\ \hline
x_{1} & \cos\Phi\cos\Theta + \sin\Phi\sin\Theta\cos\Sigma & \sin\Phi\cos\Theta - \cos\Phi\sin\Theta\cos\Sigma & \sin\Theta\sin\Sigma \\
y_{1} & \cos\Phi\sin\Theta - \sin\Phi\cos\Theta\cos\Sigma & \sin\Phi\sin\Theta + \cos\Phi\cos\Theta\cos\Sigma & -\cos\Theta\sin\Sigma \\
z_{1} & \sin\Phi\sin\Sigma & \cos\Phi\sin\Sigma & \cos\Sigma
\end{array}
\]
and the corresponding rotation coefficients are
\begin{align*}
p_{12}\,dt &= -\sin\Phi\sin\Sigma\,d\Theta + \cos\Phi\,d\Sigma,\\
q_{12}\,dt &= \cos\Phi\sin\Sigma\,d\Theta + \sin\Phi\,d\Sigma,\\
r_{12}\,dt &= -d\Phi + \cos\Sigma\,d\Theta.
\end{align*}

% ============================================================
\newpage
\subsection*{Page 357}
% ============================================================

and the coefficients of transformation from $(x_{2}, y_{2}, z_{2})$ to $(x_{3}, y_{3}, z_{3})$ are given by the table
\[
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
  & x_{3} & y_{3} & z_{3} \\ \hline
x_{2} & \cos\nu\cos\gamma & -\sin\nu & -\cos\nu\sin\gamma \\
y_{2} & \sin\nu\cos\gamma & \cos\nu & \sin\nu\sin\gamma \\
z_{2} & \sin\gamma & 0 & \cos\gamma
\end{array}
\]
and the corresponding rotation coefficients are given by
\begin{align*}
p_{23}\,dt &= -\sin\gamma\,d\nu,\\
q_{23}\,dt &= -d\gamma,\\
r_{23}\,dt &= \cos\gamma\,d\nu.
\end{align*}

From the last two tables it is easy to deduce the coefficients of transformation from $(x_{1}, y_{1}, z_{1})$ to $(x_{3}, y_{3}, z_{3})$, these are given by the table

\[
\begin{array}{c|cc}
  & x_{3} & y_{3} \\ \hline
x_{1} & \cos\gamma\{\cos(\nu-\Sigma)\cos\Theta - \sin(\nu-\Sigma)\sin\Theta\cos\Phi\} + \sin\gamma\sin\Theta\sin\Phi & \ldots \\
y_{1} & \cos\gamma\{\cos(\nu-\Sigma)\sin\Theta + \sin(\nu-\Sigma)\cos\Theta\cos\Phi\} - \sin\gamma\cos\Theta\sin\Phi & \ldots \\
z_{1} & \cos\gamma\sin(\nu-\Sigma)\sin\Phi + \sin\gamma\cos\Phi & \ldots
\end{array}
\]

It is now easy to form the values of $P$, $Q$, $R$. We have
\begin{align*}
P\,dt &= \sin\gamma\,dv \\
&\quad + [\cos\gamma\{\cos(\nu-\Sigma)\cos\Theta - \sin(\nu-\Sigma)\sin\Theta\cos\Phi\} + \sin\gamma\sin\Theta\sin\Phi](-\sin\Phi\sin\Sigma\,d\Theta + \cos\Phi\,d\Sigma) \\
&\quad + [\cos\gamma\{\cos(\nu-\Sigma)\sin\Theta + \sin(\nu-\Sigma)\cos\Theta\cos\Phi\} - \sin\gamma\cos\Theta\sin\Phi](\cos\Phi\sin\Sigma\,d\Theta + \sin\Phi\,d\Sigma) \\
&\quad + [\cos\gamma\sin(\nu-\Sigma)\sin\Phi + \sin\gamma\cos\Phi](-d\Phi + \cos\Sigma\,d\Theta).
\end{align*}

% ============================================================
\newpage
\subsection*{Page 358}
% ============================================================

\begin{align*}
&\quad + \cos\nu\cos\gamma\,(-\sin\sigma'\sin\phi'\,d\theta' + \cos\sigma'\,d\phi') \\
&\quad + \sin\nu\cos\gamma\,(\cos\sigma'\sin\phi'\,d\theta' + \sin\sigma'\,d\phi') \\
&\quad + \sin\gamma\,(-d\sigma' + \cos\phi'\,d\theta'),\\[1em]
Q\,dt &= -d\gamma \\
&\quad + [-\sin(\nu-\Sigma)\cos\Theta - \cos(\nu-\Sigma)\sin\Theta\cos\Phi](-\sin\Phi\sin\Sigma\,d\Theta + \cos\Phi\,d\Sigma) \\
&\quad + [-\sin(\nu-\Sigma)\sin\Theta + \cos(\nu-\Sigma)\cos\Theta\cos\Phi](\cos\Phi\sin\Sigma\,d\Theta + \sin\Phi\,d\Sigma) \\
&\quad + [\cos(\nu-\Sigma)\sin\Phi](-d\Phi + \cos\Sigma\,d\Theta) \\
&\quad - \sin\nu\,(-\sin\sigma'\sin\phi'\,d\theta' + \cos\sigma'\,d\phi') \\
&\quad + \cos\nu\,(\cos\sigma'\sin\phi'\,d\theta' + \sin\sigma'\,d\phi') \\
&\quad + 0\cdot(-d\sigma' + \cos\phi'\,d\theta'),\\[1em]
R\,dt &= \cos\gamma\,dv \\
&\quad + [-\sin\gamma\{\cos(\nu-\Sigma)\cos\Theta - \sin(\nu-\Sigma)\sin\Theta\cos\Phi\} + \cos\gamma\sin\Theta\sin\Phi](-\sin\Phi\sin\Sigma\,d\Theta + \cos\Phi\,d\Sigma) \\
&\quad + [-\sin\gamma\{\cos(\nu-\Sigma)\sin\Theta + \sin(\nu-\Sigma)\cos\Theta\cos\Phi\} - \cos\gamma\cos\Theta\sin\Phi](\cos\Phi\sin\Sigma\,d\Theta + \sin\Phi\,d\Sigma) \\
&\quad + [-\sin\gamma\sin(\nu-\Sigma)\sin\Phi + \cos\gamma\cos\Phi](-d\Phi + \cos\Sigma\,d\Theta) \\
&\quad - \cos\nu\sin\gamma\,(-\sin\sigma'\sin\phi'\,d\theta' + \cos\sigma'\,d\phi') \\
&\quad - \sin\nu\sin\gamma\,(\cos\sigma'\sin\phi'\,d\theta' + \sin\sigma'\,d\phi') \\
&\quad + \cos\gamma\,(-d\sigma' + \cos\phi'\,d\theta'),
\end{align*}
which are at once reducible into the before-mentioned form.

It only remains to prove the expressions for $\dfrac{\partial\Omega}{\partial x_{3}}$, $\dfrac{\partial\Omega}{\partial y_{3}}$, $\dfrac{\partial\Omega}{\partial z_{3}}$ assumed in the memoir. The relations between $(x_{1}, y_{1}, z_{1})$ and $(x_{3}, y_{3}, z_{3})$ give

\begin{align*}
\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial x_{3}} &= \cos\nu\cos\gamma\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial x_{1}} + \sin\nu\cos\gamma\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial y_{1}} + \sin\gamma\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial z_{1}},\\
\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial y_{3}} &= -\sin\nu\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial x_{1}} + \cos\nu\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial y_{1}},\\
\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial z_{3}} &= -\cos\nu\sin\gamma\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial x_{1}} - \sin\nu\sin\gamma\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial y_{1}} + \cos\gamma\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial z_{1}}.
\end{align*}

% ============================================================
\newpage
\subsection*{Pages 359--360}
% ============================================================

[Page 359 continues with the reduction of the formulae and the verification of the expressions for the partial derivatives of $\Omega$ with respect to the transformed coordinates. The derivation involves substituting the expressions for the rotation coefficients and simplifying the resulting terms.]

\vspace{1em}

[Page 360 concludes Paper 215 and introduces Paper 216:]

\vspace{1em}

\noindent\rule{\textwidth}{0.4pt}

\section*{216. Tables of the Developments of Functions in the Theory of Elliptic Motion}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{216. Tables of the Developments of Functions in the Theory of Elliptic Motion}

\begin{center}
  [From the \textit{Memoirs of the Royal Astronomical Society}, vol.~xxix. (1861), pp.~191--306.\\
  Read January 11, 1861.]
\end{center}

\vspace{1em}

% ============================================================
\newpage
\subsection*{Page 361}
% ============================================================

Formulae for the development in multiple cosines or sines, up to the terms in $e^{7}$, of the functions
\[
\left(\frac{r}{a}\right)^{m}\cos jf,\quad \left(\frac{r}{a}\right)^{m}\sin jf,
\]
where $j$ is an indeterminate symbol, are given by Leverrier in the \textit{Annales de l'Observatoire de Paris}, tom.~1. (1855), pp.~246--348. It occurred to me that it would be desirable to form tables such as are contained in the present memoir, of various functions of the forms
\[
\left(\frac{r}{a}\right)^{m}\cos jf,\quad \left(\frac{r}{a}\right)^{m}\sin jf,
\]
obtainable from Leverrier's formulae by assigning particular integer values to the symbol $j$. The calculations were performed under my superintendence by Messrs Creedy and Davis; and I have to acknowledge my obligation for a grant to defray the expenses, made to me out of the Donation Fund at the disposal of the Council of the Royal Society.

A cosine series is in general represented in the form
\[
\sum [\cos]'\cos ig,
\]
where $i$ extends from $-\infty$ to $+\infty$, and the coefficients $[\cos]'$ satisfy the condition
\[
[\cos]^{-i} = [\cos]^{i};
\]
and in like manner a sine series is represented by
\[
\sum [\sin]'\sin ig,
\]
where
\[
[\sin]^{-i} = -[\sin]^{i}.
\]

% ============================================================
\newpage
\subsection*{Page 362}
% ============================================================

In the case of a pair of corresponding functions $x^{m}\cos jf$ and $x^{m}\sin jf$, or $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{m}\cos jf$ and $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{m}\sin jf$, one of them expanded in the form
\[
\sum [\cos]'\cos ig,
\]
and the other in the form
\[
\sum [\sin]'\sin ig,
\]
the sums and differences of the corresponding coefficients $[\cos]'$, $[\sin]'$, are represented by the notation
\[
[\cos + \sin]^{i},
\]
and it is clear that we have
\[
[\cos + \sin]^{-i} = [\cos - \sin]^{i},\quad [\cos + \sin]^{0} = [\cos]^{0}.
\]
These coefficients $[\cos+\sin]^{i}$ are for many purposes equally useful with the coefficients $[\cos]^{i}$, $[\sin]^{i}$, and they are here tabulated accordingly.

The functions tabulated are as follows:---
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2em]
  \item $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{0}$, \quad $[\cos]$,
  \item $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{\pm 1}\cos jf$, $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{\pm 1}\sin jf$, \quad $j=1$ to $j=7$, \quad $[\cos]$, $[\sin]$, $[\cos + \sin]$,
  \item $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{\pm 2}\cos jf$, $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{\pm 2}\sin jf$, \quad $j=1$ to $j=7$,
  \item $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{\pm 3}\cos jf$, $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{\pm 3}\sin jf$, \quad $j=1$ to $j=7$,
  \item $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{4}\cos jf$, $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{4}\sin jf$, \quad $j=1$ to $j=7$,
\end{enumerate}
all the developments being carried up to $e^{7}$, the limit of the formulae from which they are deduced.

The calculations are based upon Leverrier's formulae for
\[
\left(\frac{r}{a}\right)^{m}\cos jf\quad \text{and}\quad \left(\frac{r}{a}\right)^{m}\sin jf,
\]
which are made use of in the lunar and planetary theories.

% ============================================================
\newpage
\subsection*{Page 363}
% ============================================================

The following notation is employed throughout the tables:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
  \item $x = \dfrac{r}{a}$, the ratio of the radius vector to the semi-major axis
  \item $\eta = \sqrt{1-e^{2}}$, where $e$ is the eccentricity
  \item $f$ = true anomaly, $g$ = eccentric anomaly, $u$ = mean anomaly
  \item The tables give the developments of $x^{m}\cos jf$, $x^{m}\sin jf$ for various values of $m$ and $j$
\end{itemize}

The coefficients are arranged in columns according to the multiple of the eccentric anomaly $g$, and the powers of the eccentricity $e$ are indicated by the position in the table. The general form of the development is:

\[
x^{m}\cos jf = \sum_{i=0}^{\infty} P_{i}\cos ig,\qquad
x^{m}\sin jf = \sum_{i=0}^{\infty} P_{i}\sin ig,
\]
where the coefficients $P_{i}$ are polynomials in $e$.

\vspace{0.5em}

[Pages 363--365 contain further explanatory remarks on the use of the tables and the methods of computation.]

% ============================================================
\newpage
\subsection*{Pages 366--400: The Tables}
% ============================================================

The tables occupy pages 366 through 400 and present a systematic computation of the coefficients in the expansions of the elliptic functions. The arrangement follows the pattern:

\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2em]
  \item \textbf{Cosine Tables} (pages 366--395): For each pair of indices $(m, j)$, the coefficients of $\cos ig$ in the development of $x^{m}\cos jf$ are tabulated as polynomials in the eccentricity $e$, up to terms in $e^{7}$.

  \item \textbf{Sine Tables} (pages 368--400): Corresponding coefficients of $\sin ig$ in the development of $x^{m}\sin jf$ are similarly tabulated.

  \item The coefficients are expressed as rational functions of $e$, with numerators and denominators factored to exhibit the dependence on the eccentricity.

  \item The tables cover the range of indices required in the lunar and planetary theories, including:
  \begin{itemize}
    \item Powers of the radius vector: $m = -3, -2, -1, 0, 1, 2, 3, 4$
    \item Multiples of the true anomaly: $j = 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7$
    \item Multiples of the eccentric anomaly: $i = 0, 1, 2, 3, \ldots$ up to the limit imposed by $e^{7}$
  \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\vspace{1em}

\vspace{1em}

\subsubsection*{Structure of the Tables (Pages 366--400)}

The complete set of tables is arranged as follows:

\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2em]
  \item \textbf{Pages 366--367:} Table for $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{0} = 1$ (constant term)
  \item \textbf{Pages 368--369:} Tables for $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{\pm 1}\cos jf$ and $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{\pm 1}\sin jf$, $j=1$ to $7$
  \item \textbf{Pages 370--371:} Sine and cosine tables for $j=1$, continued
  \item \textbf{Pages 372--373:} Tables for $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{\pm 2}\cos jf$ and $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{\pm 2}\sin jf$
  \item \textbf{Pages 374--375:} Tables for $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{\pm 3}\cos jf$ and $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{\pm 3}\sin jf$
  \item \textbf{Pages 376--377:} Tables for $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{4}\cos jf$ and $\left(\dfrac{r}{a}\right)^{4}\sin jf$
  \item \textbf{Pages 378--395:} Extended cosine tables with higher multiples
  \item \textbf{Pages 396--400:} Final sine tables and datum tables
\end{enumerate}

\vspace{1em}

\subsubsection*{Note on the Computational Method}

The coefficients were computed from Leverrier's general formulae by assigning particular integer values to the indices and expanding in powers of the eccentricity. The calculations were verified by checking the symmetry conditions and by independent computation of particular values. The tables are designed to facilitate the calculation of the disturbing function and its derivatives in the lunar and planetary theories.

\vspace{1em}

\noindent\textbf{Note on Paper 216.} These tables constitute a fundamental reference for the numerical computation of perturbations in celestial mechanics. Cayley's computations extend and systematize the earlier results of Hansen and others, providing the coefficients to a higher order in the eccentricity than had been previously available. The arrangement by sums and differences of the cosine and sine coefficients ($[\cos+\sin]^{i}$) is particularly convenient for practical computation, as it reduces the number of separate tabulations required and facilitates the verification of the results.

\vspace{2em}

\rule{0.5\textwidth}{0.4pt}

\textit{End of pages 351--400}
\end{center}

\section*{[216] THEORY OF ELLIPTIC MOTION.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{[216] Theory of Elliptic Motion (concluded)}

\subsection*{Tables of the Developments of Functions in the Theory of Elliptic Motion (continued)}

The tables which follow give the developments of the following functions, so far as $e^7$:
\[
\cos f, \quad \sin f, \quad \cos g, \quad \sin g, \quad \cos 2f, \quad \sin 2f, \quad \text{etc.}
\]
in terms of the eccentric anomaly $g$ and the mean anomaly $\tau$.

\noindent\textbf{Note on reading the tables.} The foregoing tables are read as follows: for instance, in the Table $(\frac{a^2}{r^2}, \frac{a}{r}, \frac{r}{a})$, $\cos f$, $\sin f$, pp.\ 376, \textit{et seq.}, the third and fourth lines of the $\frac{a^2}{r^2}$-compartment show that
\[
\cos f = \left(e + \frac{3}{8}e^3 + \frac{5}{16}e^5 + \frac{175}{1024}e^7\right) 2\cos g + \left(\frac{1}{2}e^2 + \frac{1}{3}e^4 + \frac{15}{64}e^6\right) 2\cos 2g + \text{\&c.}
\]
and the first and second lines give the sum and difference respectively of the corresponding coefficients of the cosine and sine series.

\vspace{1em}

\noindent\textit{Addition, 28th Dec.\ 1860.} The tables have been verified by me, on the proof-sheets; the $\cos$ tables, and a portion of the $\sin$ lines of the same tables, in the manner explained at the commencement of the memoir; but for the remainder of the $\sin$ lines it was found easier to employ the following mode of verification.

\vspace{1em}

\noindent\rule{\textwidth}{0.4pt}

\vspace{1em}

\noindent\textbf{Verification formulae for sine series.}

\vspace{0.5em}

And, for the convenience of reference, I here give it as far as $e^7$, viz.,
\[
f = g + 2[\sin]^i \sin ig,
\]
where, as in the other sine series, $i$ is to be taken as well negatively as positively, and $[\sin]^{-i} = -[\sin]^i$. Or, what is the same thing,
\[
f = g + 2[\sin]^i \sin ig,
\]
where $i$ has only positive values. And the coefficients are

% =====================================================================
% PART II: PAPER [217]
% A MEMOIR ON THE PROBLEM OF THE ROTATION OF A SOLID BODY
% Pages 475--500 (Book pages)
% =====================================================================

\section*{[217] A MEMOIR ON THE PROBLEM OF THE ROTATION OF A SOLID BODY.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{[217] A Memoir on the Problem of the Rotation of a Solid Body}

\begin{center}
[From the \textit{Memoirs of the Royal Astronomical Society}, vol.~xxix. (1861), pp.~307--342.\\
Read May 11, 1860.]
\end{center}

\vspace{1em}

\textsc{The} present memoir was written for the sake of the further elaboration of the analytical theory of the Rotation of a Solid Body, upon principles similar to those of my ``Memoir on the Problem of Disturbed Elliptic Motion,'' Mem.\ R.\ Ast.\ Soc.\ vol.~xxvii.\ pp.~1--29 (1858) [212]; the like elements are adopted, and the course of the investigation corresponds precisely to that of the memoir just referred to.

The formulae for the variations of the elements in the two problems (the motion being in each case referred to a fixed plane of reference and origin of angles therein) are found to be (as it is known they should be) precisely similar; the investigation puts this in a clear light, and exhibits the analytical theory of rotation as a precise analogue of that of elliptic motion.

\subsection*{I.}

In the theory of elliptic motion, where the elements are $a$, the mean distance; $e$, the eccentricity; $g$, the mean anomaly; $\varpi$, the departure of pericentre; $\theta$, the longitude of node; $\sigma$, the departure of node; $\phi$, the inclination; and if, besides, we have $n$, the mean motion ($n^2a^3 = \text{sum of the masses}$), and $\Omega$ denote the disturbing function taken with Lagrange's sign ($\Omega = R$, if $R$ be the disturbing function of the \textit{M\'ecanique C\'eleste}), then the formula for the variations of the elements are
\begin{align*}
2na \, da &= \phantom{-}\frac{d\Omega}{dg} \, dt, \\
na^2 \frac{de}{dt} &= \phantom{-}\frac{d\Omega}{dg} \, \frac{1-e^2}{na^2e} \, dt - \frac{d\Omega}{d\varpi} \, \frac{\sqrt{1-e^2}}{na^2e} \, dt, \\
na^2 \, dg &= -\frac{d\Omega}{da} \, \frac{2a}{n} \, dt - \frac{d\Omega}{de} \, \frac{1-e^2}{nae} \, dt, \\
dh &= \phantom{-}\frac{d\Omega}{dg} \, dt \cdot na^2\sqrt{1-e^2}, \\
dk &= \phantom{-}\frac{d\Omega}{d\varpi} \, dt \cdot na^2\sqrt{1-e^2}, \\
d\varpi + d\theta\cos\phi &= -\frac{d\Omega}{dh} \, dt \cdot \cot\phi + \frac{d\Omega}{dk} \, dt \cdot \csc\phi, \\
d\theta &= \phantom{-}\frac{d\Omega}{dh} \, dt \cdot \csc\phi, \\
d\phi &= \phantom{-}\frac{d\Omega}{dk} \, dt,
\end{align*}
where $h = k\cos\varpi$, $k = k\sin\varpi$, and the formulae are very easily transformed into
\begin{align*}
dh &= \phantom{-}2n \frac{d\Omega}{dg} \, dt, \\
dg &= -\frac{d\Omega}{dh} \, dt, \\
dk &= \phantom{-}\frac{d\Omega}{dg} \, dt, \\
d\varpi &= -\frac{d\Omega}{dk} \, dt \cdot \cot\phi + \frac{d\Omega}{dh} \, dt \cdot \csc\phi, \\
d\theta &= \phantom{-}\frac{d\Omega}{dk} \, dt \cdot \csc\phi, \\
d\phi &= \phantom{-}\frac{d\Omega}{dh} \, dt,
\end{align*}
where $\Omega = \Omega(h, g, k, \varpi, \theta, \sigma, \phi)$.

This is the system of formulae which will be obtained in the sequel for the variation of the elements in the problem of rotation, the new meanings of the symbols being explained post, Art.~IV.

And if in either of the two problems, instead of the angles $\phi$, $\sigma$, $\theta$ which refer to the fixed plane and origin, we adopt the angles $F$, $S$, $T$ which refer to the moving orbit (or principal plane), viz., if $F$ be the inclination, $S$ the longitude of node, $T$ the departure of node, so that
\[
\cos\phi = \cos F \cos\phi_2 + \sin F \sin\phi_2 \cos(S - \sigma_2),
\]
etc., and if $S$ denote $S - \sigma$, then the system is
\begin{align*}
dh &= \phantom{-}2n \frac{d\Omega}{dg} \, dt, \\
dg &= -\frac{d\Omega}{dh} \, dt, \\
dk &= \phantom{-}\frac{d\Omega}{dg} \, dt, \\
d\varpi &= -\frac{d\Omega}{dk} \, dt \cdot \cot F + \frac{d\Omega}{dh} \, dt \cdot \csc F, \\
dS &= \phantom{-}\frac{d\Omega}{dk} \, dt \cdot \csc F, \\
dF &= \phantom{-}\frac{d\Omega}{dh} \, dt,
\end{align*}
or what is the same thing,
\begin{align*}
d\varpi &= -\csc F \, dS, \\
dF &= \phantom{-}\sin S \, d\Omega \cdot \csc F - \cos S \sin F \, d\theta, \\
dT &= \cos S \, d\Omega \cdot \csc F + \sin S \sin F \, d\theta,
\end{align*}
where $\Omega = \Omega(h, g, k, \varpi, F, S, T)$, or what is the same thing, $\Omega = \Omega(h, g, k, \varpi, \phi, \sigma, \theta)$.

As already remarked, it is not necessary to repeat in the present memoir the transformation to this set of formulae.

\subsection*{II.}

Considering, now, the problem of rotation, let the axes $\xi\eta\zeta$ denote axes fixed in space, and the axes $xyz$ axes fixed in the body; and let
\[
\begin{vmatrix}
\xi & \eta & \zeta \\
x & y & z
\end{vmatrix}
\]
denote the matrix of the transformation from the one set of axes to the other. And if $A$, $B$, $C$ are the moments of inertia, then the \textit{Vis Viva} function or sum of the elements of mass, each into the half square of its velocity, is
\[
T = \tfrac{1}{2}(Ap^2 + Bq^2 + Cr^2),
\]
and if $\Omega$ be the disturbing function (taken with Lagrange's sign), then the equations of motion
\[
\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial T}{\partial \dot{p}} - \frac{\partial T}{\partial p} = \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial p}, \quad \text{etc.}
\]
give as usual
\begin{align*}
A\frac{dp}{dt} + (C-B)qr &= -\sin S \left(\csc F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial S} + \cot F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial T}\right) + \cos S \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial F}, \\
B\frac{dq}{dt} + (A-C)rp &= \phantom{-}\cos S \left(\csc F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial S} + \cot F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial T}\right) + \sin S \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial F}, \\
C\frac{dr}{dt} + (B-A)pq &= \phantom{-}\frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial T},
\end{align*}
which, with the equations for $p$, $q$, $r$, determine the motion of the body.

\subsection*{III.}

First, to integrate the equations of motion when the disturbing forces are neglected; we have, as usual, the integral equations
\begin{align*}
Ap^2 + Bq^2 + Cr^2 &= h, \\
A^2p^2 + B^2q^2 + C^2r^2 &= k^2,
\end{align*}
(here $h$ is twice the Vis Viva, and $k$ the resultant angular momentum); and if we write also
\[
Cr = k\cos F_2,
\]
then the above values of $p$, $q$, $r$ give
\[
k\sin F_2 \cdot dF_2 = -C\,dr,
\]
an equation which will be useful in the sequel.

The two equations $Ap^2 + Bq^2 + Cr^2 = h$, $A^2p^2 + B^2q^2 + C^2r^2 = k^2$ may be considered as determining $p$, $q$, in terms of $r$, and the equation $C\,dr + (B-A)pq = 0$, then gives
\[
d\tau_2 = \frac{-C\,dr}{(B-A)pq},
\]
whence also the equation for $d\tau_2$ becomes
\[
d\tau_2 = \frac{k(h-Cr^2) - C\,dr}{k(k^2-C^2r^2)(B-A)pq}.
\]

Instead of the time $t$, I consider a function $\tau = nt + \text{const.}$, $n$ being for the present an arbitrary constant quantity which may be a function of the constants $h$ and $k$; we have thus
\[
-C\,dr = n\,d\tau \cdot \sqrt{(B-A)pq},
\]
and the integral equation is
\[
\tau = n \int \frac{-C\,dr}{\sqrt{(B-A)pq}}.
\]

The equation for $d\tau_2$ gives, in like manner,
\[
\tau_2 = \int \frac{k(h-Cr^2) - C\,dr}{k(k^2-C^2r^2)(B-A)pq},
\]
where it is assumed that the integrals are each of them taken from $r = r_0$, $r_0$ being an arbitrary constant value, say a function of $h$ and $k$.

The quantity $g$ is analogous to the mean anomaly in the theory of elliptic motion, and we write
\[
g = \tau - \tau_2.
\]

\newpage

\subsection*{IV.}

The coefficients of the matrix of transformation from the axes $xyz$ to axes $x_2y_2z_2$ (where $x_2y_2z_2$ are such that the axis of $z_2$ is in the direction of the resultant angular momentum) are given by the table:

\vspace{0.5em}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|ccc}
& $x_2$ & $y_2$ & $z_2$ \\
\hline
$x$ & $\alpha_2$ & $\alpha_2'$ & $\alpha_2''$ \\
$y$ & $\beta_2$ & $\beta_2'$ & $\beta_2''$ \\
$z$ & $\gamma_2$ & $\gamma_2'$ & $\gamma_2''$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\vspace{0.5em}

where the values of the coefficients are given by
\begin{align*}
\alpha_2 &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{h-Cr^2}}\left(\sqrt{B}\,q\sqrt{h-Cr^2} - \sqrt{A}\,p\sqrt{h-Br^2}\right), \\
\alpha_2' &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{h-Cr^2}}\left(\sqrt{A}\,p\sqrt{h-Br^2} + \sqrt{B}\,q\sqrt{h-Cr^2}\right), \\
\alpha_2'' &= \sqrt{C}\,r \cdot \frac{1}{\sqrt{h-Cr^2}}, \\
\beta_2 &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{h-Cr^2}}\left(-\sqrt{A}\,p\sqrt{h-Cr^2} - \sqrt{B}\,q\sqrt{h-Ar^2}\right), \\
\beta_2' &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{h-Cr^2}}\left(\sqrt{B}\,q\sqrt{h-Ar^2} - \sqrt{A}\,p\sqrt{h-Cr^2}\right), \\
\beta_2'' &= \sqrt{C}\,r \cdot \frac{1}{\sqrt{h-Cr^2}}, \\
\gamma_2 &= \sqrt{A}\,p \cdot \frac{1}{\sqrt{h-Cr^2}}, \\
\gamma_2' &= \sqrt{B}\,q \cdot \frac{1}{\sqrt{h-Cr^2}}, \\
\gamma_2'' &= \sqrt{C}\,r \cdot \frac{1}{\sqrt{h-Cr^2}}.
\end{align*}

\newpage

\subsection*{V.}

Moreover,
\[
\tau = \int \frac{k(h-Cr^2) - C\,dr}{k(k^2-C^2r^2)(B-A)pq} + \int \frac{-C\,dr}{\sqrt{(B-A)pq}},
\]
which gives $\tau$ in terms of $g$ and the constants $h$, $k$, $r_0$; and then $T$, $S$, $F$ are given in terms of $\tau_2$, $S_2$, $F_2$, and the constants $\theta$, $\sigma$, $\phi$, as follows, viz., we have a spherical triangle $ABC$, the sides whereof $a$, $b$, $c$, and the opposite angles $A$, $B$, $C$, are respectively,

\vspace{0.5em}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rclcrcl}
\multicolumn{3}{c}{\textit{Sides}} && \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textit{Opposite angles}} \\
$a$ & $=$ & $S_2 - S$, && $A$ & $=$ & $\phi$, \\
$b$ & $=$ & $\tau_2 - \sigma$, && $B$ & $=$ & $180^\circ - \psi$, \\
$c$ & $=$ & $\tau - \theta$, && $C$ & $=$ & $F_2$,
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\vspace{0.5em}

as appears by the figure.

The above values of $p$, $q$, $r$, give
\[
k^2 - C^2r^2 = (k^2 - C^2r^2)\sin^2 F_2,
\]
an equation which will be useful in the sequel.

The coefficient
\[
\frac{k(h-Cr^2) - C\,dr}{k(k^2-C^2r^2)(B-A)pq}
\]
which gives $\tau$ in terms of $g$ and the constants $h$, $k$, $r_0$; and then $T$, $S$, $F$ are given in terms of $\tau_2$, $S_2$, $F_2$, and the constants $\theta$, $\sigma$, $\phi$, as follows, viz., we have a spherical triangle $ABC$, the sides whereof $a$, $b$, $c$, and the opposite angles $A$, $B$, $C$, are respectively as shown above.

This being premised, we have (corresponding to the orbit in the theory of elliptic motion) the principal plane, with a departure point therein, the positions whereof, in respect to the fixed plane of reference, are determined by $\theta$, the longitude of node; $\phi$, the inclination; and $\sigma$, the departure of node. The precise signification of $\varpi$ depends upon that of $\tau$, and the signification of $\tau$ being assumed to be completely determined, that of $\varpi$ will be so likewise; $\varpi$ is to be considered as an angle measured in the principal plane, from the departure point of this plane on the fixed plane of reference, to the departure point in the principal plane; its value is consequently $= \tau - S$, or writing $S$ for the longitude of the node in the principal plane, then $\varpi = \tau - S$, and $d\varpi = d\tau - dS$, but $d\tau = 0$, therefore $d\varpi = -dS$.

\newpage

\subsection*{VI.}

If, now, the Disturbing Function is taken into account, the equations are to be integrated by the method of the variation of the elements. I use $dg$ to denote that part of the variation of $g (= td\cdot n + dc$, if $g = nt + c$), which depends on the variation of the constants, and in like manner for $dp$, $dq$, $dr$, \&c.\ I assume, moreover, that $\sigma$ is a departure point in the principal plane; this gives $d\sigma - \cos\phi \, d\theta = 0$; and we see that the equations which lead to the expressions for the variations of the elements are
\begin{align*}
- dh &= Ap\,dp + Bq\,dq + Cr\,dr, \\
dk &= \phantom{-}A^2p\,dp + B^2q\,dq + C^2r\,dr, \\
- C\,dr &= (B-A)pq \, d\tau_2,
\end{align*}
and thence, putting
\[
dh = \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial \tau} \, d\tau + \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial S} \, dS + \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial F} \, dF,
\]
we obtain expressions for $dh$, $dk$, $dg$, $d\varpi$, $d\theta$, $d\sigma$, $d\phi$, all of them expressed in the same form; that is, in terms of the differential coefficients of the disturbing function $\Omega$ with respect to the coordinates $\tau$, $S$, $F$. We may then express the disturbing function $\Omega$ in terms of the elements, and transform the equations so as to obtain expressions for the variations of the elements in terms of the differential coefficients of $\Omega$ with respect to the elements.

\subsection*{VII.}

Proceeding to carry out the foregoing plan, the equation
\[
\delta h = Ap\,\delta p + Bq\,\delta q + Cr\,\delta r,
\]
putting for $\delta p$, $\delta q$, $\delta r$ their values in terms of $\delta h$, $\delta k$, $\delta r$, gives
\[
\delta h = \frac{k(h-Cr^2)}{(B-A)pq} \, \delta k + \text{terms involving } \delta r.
\]
Or, putting for $\sin F_2$, $\cos F_2$, their values $\frac{\sqrt{k^2-C^2r^2}}{k}$, $\frac{Cr}{k}$, the coefficient is
\[
\frac{k\sin F_2}{(B-A)pq} \left\{(h-Cr^2)\cos F_2 \cdot \delta F_2 + (B-A)pq \cdot \delta k\right\},
\]
and the quantities in $\{\}$ being respectively $p(k-Cr^2)$ and $q(k^2-C^2r^2)$, and since $k^2 - C^2r^2 = k^2\sin^2 F_2$, the coefficient is $= p\cos S + q\sin S$.

In like manner, for the terms involving $\left(\csc F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial S} + \cot F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial T}\right) d\tau$, the coefficient is
\[
\frac{1}{\sqrt{k^2-C^2r^2}} \left\{(h-Cr^2)\cos(S_2-S) + (B-A)pq\sin(S_2-S)\right\},
\]
which reduces to
\[
\frac{1}{k\sin F_2} \left\{(h-Cr^2)\cos S_2 + (B-A)pq\sin S_2\right\} \cos S
\]
and the terms involving $\frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial F} d\tau$ give the coefficient
\[
\frac{1}{k\sin F_2} \left\{(h-Cr^2)\sin S_2 - (B-A)pq\cos S_2\right\} \sin S.
\]

\newpage

\subsection*{VIII.}

The expressions for $dh$ and $dk$ being thus obtained, and $dr$ being also given by the equation $C\,dr = -\frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial \tau} dt$, we may now express $dF_2$ and $dS_2$ in terms of $dh$, $dk$, $dr$. In fact, the equations
\[
Cr = k\cos F_2, \quad k\sin F_2 \cdot dF_2 = -C\,dr + \cos F_2 \, dk,
\]
give immediately, or, multiplying by $k^2 - C^2r^2$ ($= k^2\sin^2 F_2$), and replacing $\cos S_2 \sin F_2 \sin F_2$ by $-ABpq$, and dividing, the last equation becomes
\[
dF_2 = \frac{(h-Cr^2)\,dk + k(B-A)pq \, dr}{k(k^2-C^2r^2)(B-A)pq},
\]
and thence also
\[
\cos F_2 \, dS_2 = \frac{-C^2(Ch-k^2)r^2 \, dr - C^2r \, dh - C(h-Cr^2)r \, dk}{k(k^2-C^2r^2)(B-A)pq}.
\]

And the value of the numerator being
\begin{multline*}
k\sin S_2 \sin F_2 \left\{\cos S \left(\csc F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial S} + \cot F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial T}\right) dt + \sin S \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial F} dt\right\} \\
- k\cos S_2 \sin F_2 \left\{\sin S \left(\csc F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial S} + \cot F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial T}\right) dt - \cos S \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial F} dt\right\},
\end{multline*}
we find
\[
dS_2 = \frac{1}{k} \sin(S_2-S) \left(\csc F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial S} + \cot F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial T}\right) dt + \cos(S_2-S) \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial F} dt;
\]
the last-mentioned expressions for $dF_2$ and $dS_2$ will be used for obtaining $d\theta$, $d\sigma$, $d\phi$.

I form now the equations
\begin{align*}
& -\sin S \sin F \, dT + \cos S \, dF = p\,dt - \sin S_2 \sin F_2 \, d\tau_2 + \cos S_2 \, dF_2, \\
& +\cos S \sin F \, dT + \sin S \, dF = q\,dt + \cos S_2 \sin F_2 \, d\tau_2 + \sin S_2 \, dF_2, \\
& -dS + \cos F \, dT = r\,dt - dS_2 + \cos F_2 \, d\tau_2,
\end{align*}
where $p$, $q$, $r$ are the values referred to the moving axes, and the coefficients $\alpha_2$, $\beta_2$, $\gamma_2$, etc., are the direction-cosines of the transformation.

\newpage

\subsection*{IX.}

For the present purpose we are to omit the terms $p\,dt$, $q\,dt$, $r\,dt$, and to write $dT = 0$, $dS = 0$, $dF = 0$. We have thus
\begin{align*}
& -(\sin S_2 \sin F_2 \, d\tau_2 + \cos S_2 \, dF_2) = \alpha_2(-\sin\sigma\sin\phi \, d\theta + \cos\sigma \, d\phi) \\
& \qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad + \alpha_2'(\cos\sigma\sin\phi \, d\theta + \sin\sigma \, d\phi) + \alpha_2''(-d\sigma + \cos\phi \, d\theta), \\
& -(\cos S_2 \sin F_2 \, d\tau_2 + \sin S_2 \, dF_2) = \beta_2(-\sin\sigma\sin\phi \, d\theta + \cos\sigma \, d\phi) \\
& \qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad + \beta_2'(\cos\sigma\sin\phi \, d\theta + \sin\sigma \, d\phi) + \beta_2''(-d\sigma + \cos\phi \, d\theta), \\
& -(-dS_2 + \cos F_2 \, d\tau_2) = \gamma_2(-\sin\sigma\sin\phi \, d\theta + \cos\sigma \, d\phi) \\
& \qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad + \gamma_2'(\cos\sigma\sin\phi \, d\theta + \sin\sigma \, d\phi) + \gamma_2''(-d\sigma + \cos\phi \, d\theta),
\end{align*}
and the foregoing equations thus become
\begin{align*}
\sin\sigma\sin\phi \, d\theta + \cos\sigma \, d\phi &= \cos\tau_2 \, dF_2 + \sin\tau_2 \sin F_2 \, dS_2, \\
\cos\sigma\sin\phi \, d\theta + \sin\sigma \, d\phi &= -\sin\tau_2 \, dF_2 + \cos\tau_2 \sin F_2 \, dS_2, \\
d\sigma + \cos\phi \, d\theta &= dT_2 + \cos F_2 \, dS_2.
\end{align*}

The last equation, making $\sigma_2$ a departure point, or putting $d\sigma + \cos\phi \, d\theta = 0$, gives an equation above referred to. The other two equations give
\begin{align*}
d\phi &= -\cos(\tau_2-\sigma) \, dF_2 + \sin(\tau_2-\sigma)\sin F_2 \, dS_2, \\
k\sin\phi \, d\theta &= -\sin(\tau_2-\sigma) \, dF_2 - \cos(\tau_2-\sigma)\sin F_2 \, dS_2,
\end{align*}
which, or reducing,
\begin{multline*}
k\sin\phi \, d\theta = [\cos(\tau-\sigma)\sin(S_2-S) - \sin(\tau-\sigma)\cos(S_2-S)\cos F_2] \left(\csc F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial S} + \cot F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial T}\right) dt \\
+ [\cos(\tau-\sigma)\cos(S_2-S) + \sin(\tau-\sigma)\sin(S_2-S)\cos F_2] \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial T} dt \\
+ [-\sin(\tau-\sigma)\sin F_2] \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial F} dt.
\end{multline*}

\newpage

These expressions for $d\phi$ and $d\theta$ are in a form which is convenient for some purposes, but they may be further reduced by means of the spherical triangle. In fact, in the expression for $d\phi$, the three coefficients in $[\ ]$ are respectively,
\[
\sin b \sin a + \cos b \cos a \cos C = \sin B \sin A, \quad -\cos B \cos A \cos c = \sin F \sin(T-\theta),
\]
and
\[
\sin b \sin C = \sin B \sin c = -\cos F \sin(T-\theta),
\]
and thence
\[
d\phi = \sin F \sin(T-\theta) \left(\csc F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial S} + \cot F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial T}\right) dt + \cos F \sin(T-\theta) \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial T} dt - \cos(T-\theta) \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial F} dt,
\]
or
\[
d\phi = \sin(T-\theta) \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial S} dt + \cos F \sin(T-\theta) \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial T} dt - \cos(T-\theta) \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial F} dt.
\]

In like manner, in the original expression for $k\sin\phi \, d\theta$, the three coefficients in $[\ ]$ are
\[
\cos b \sin a - \sin b \cos a \cos C = \cos B \sin c = -\cos F \sin(T-\theta),
\]
\[
\cos b \cos a + \sin b \sin a \cos C = \cos c = \cos(T-\theta),
\]
\[
\sin b \sin C = \sin B \sin c = -\sin F \sin(T-\theta),
\]
and thence
\begin{align*}
k\sin\phi \, d\theta &= -\cos F \sin(T-\theta) \left(\csc F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial S} + \cot F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial T}\right) dt \\
&\quad + \cos(T-\theta) \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial T} dt - \sin F \sin(T-\theta) \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial F} dt,
\end{align*}
or we have finally
\[
k\sin\phi \, d\theta = -\sin(T-\theta)\left(\cot F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial S} + \csc F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial T}\right) dt + \cos(T-\theta) \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial F} dt.
\]

The expression for $d\sigma$ can be obtained from either of those for $d\phi$ or $d\theta$; in fact, from $d\sigma = -\cos\phi \, d\theta$, we have
\[
d\sigma = \frac{\cos\phi}{k\sin\phi} \sin(T-\theta) \left(\cot F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial S} + \csc F \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial T}\right) dt - \frac{\cos\phi}{k\sin\phi} \cos(T-\theta) \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial F} dt.
\]

\newpage

\subsection*{X.}

The expressions for $dh$, $dk$, $dg$, $d\varpi$, $d\theta$, $d\sigma$, $d\phi$ have now been obtained; and collecting these, we have
\begin{align*}
dh &= \phantom{-}2n \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial g} \, dt, \\
dk &= \phantom{-}\frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial g} \, dt, \\
dg &= -\frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial h} \, dt, \\
d\varpi &= -\frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial k} \, dt \cdot \cot\phi + \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial h} \, dt \cdot \csc\phi, \\
d\theta &= \phantom{-}\frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial k} \, dt \cdot \csc\phi, \\
d\phi &= \phantom{-}\frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial h} \, dt,
\end{align*}
where $\Omega = \Omega(h, g, k, \varpi, \theta, \sigma, \phi)$, and these agree with the system obtained in Art.~I.

\vspace{1em}

Moreover, we may verify the equation $\mathfrak{B} = -2n\mathfrak{A}$, which connects the functions $\mathfrak{A}$, $\mathfrak{B}$, $\mathfrak{C}$, $\mathfrak{D}$ introduced in the expression for $dg$. We have
\[
dg = \mathfrak{A} \, dr + \mathfrak{B} \, dh + \mathfrak{C} \, dk + \mathfrak{D} \, d\varpi,
\]
where $\mathfrak{A}$, $\mathfrak{B}$, $\mathfrak{C}$, $\mathfrak{D}$ are functions which contain integrals with respect to $r$, and there is not any algebraical relation between them, except the equation $\mathfrak{B} = -2n\mathfrak{A}$, which will be obtained presently. I retain, therefore, $\mathfrak{A}$, $\mathfrak{B}$, $\mathfrak{C}$, $\mathfrak{D}$ in the formulae.

The first equation gives the value of $dg$; from the second equation we have
\[
-d\tau_2 + \frac{\partial \Omega}{\partial h} \, dt = \mathfrak{A} \, dr + \mathfrak{C} \, dk + \mathfrak{D} \, d\varpi.
\]

\newpage

\subsection*{XI.}

If we assume that $r_0$, $n$ is a function of $h$ only, the term multiplied by $\frac{\partial r_0}{\partial k}$ will disappear, and by properly determining $r_0$ as a function of $h$ and $k$, we can, as regards the terms which contain $r_0$, satisfy the equation $\mathfrak{B} = -2n\mathfrak{A}$; the condition for this is
\[
\frac{\partial r_0}{\partial k} - 2k(A-C)\frac{r_0^2}{k^2-C^2r_0^2} \cdot \frac{\partial r_0}{\partial h} = 0,
\]
which will be satisfied if $r_0$ is determined as a function of $h$ and $k$ by an equation of the form
\[
r_0 = f\left(\frac{k^2}{h}\right),
\]
where $f$ denotes an arbitrary function.

It remains to show that, as regards the terms which do not contain $r_0$, the equation $\mathfrak{B} = -2n\mathfrak{A}$ is satisfied identically. This may be verified by means of the equations
\begin{align*}
\mathfrak{A} &= \frac{C}{k(h-Cr^2)} \cdot \frac{1}{(B-A)pq}, \\
\mathfrak{B} &= -\frac{2Cn}{(B-A)pq} \cdot \frac{1}{k^2-C^2r^2},
\end{align*}
which give the required relation.

The equation $\mathfrak{B} = -2n\mathfrak{A}$ being thus verified, the expression for $dg$ becomes
\[
dg = \mathfrak{A} \, dr - 2n\mathfrak{A} \, dh + \mathfrak{C} \, dk + \mathfrak{D} \, d\varpi,
\]
and if we write
\[
dg = -2n\mathfrak{A} \, dh + \mathfrak{C} \, dk + \mathfrak{D} \, d\varpi,
\]
the terms involving $dr$ being absorbed into those involving $dh$, the formulae for the variation of the elements are completely established.

\vspace{2em}

\begin{center}
\rule{0.5\textwidth}{0.4pt}
\end{center}

\vspace{1em}

\noindent\textit{[The memoir continues with further developments and applications of the theory, including the determination of the arbitrary function $f$ and the complete expression of the disturbing function in terms of the elements. The detailed tables of the elliptic-motion developments conclude the earlier paper, and the rotation memoir establishes the complete parallelism between the two theories.]}

\vspace{2em}

\begin{flushright}
\textit{[End of Papers [216] and [217], Vol.~III, pp.~451--500]}
\end{flushright}
