% PAPER 221
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\setcounter{section}{220}
\section{ON THE SECULAR ACCELERATION OF THE MOON'S MEAN MOTION}
\markboth{On the Secular Acceleration of the Moon's Mean Motion.}{}

\begin{center}
[From the \textit{Monthly Notices of the Royal Astronomical Society}, vol.\ xxii.\ (1862), pp.~171--231.]
\end{center}

The present Memoir exhibits a new method of taking account, in the Lunar Theory, of the variation of the eccentricity of the Sun's orbit.

The approximation is carried to the same extent as in Prof.\ Adams' Memoir ``On the Secular Variation of the Moon's Mean Motion'' (Phil.\ Trans., vol.\ CXLIII.\ (1853), pp.~397--406); and I obtain results agreeing precisely with his, viz., besides his new periodic terms in the longitude and radius vector, I obtain in the longitude the secular term
\[
\left(\tfrac{3}{4}m^2 - \tfrac{81}{32}m^4\right)\int\frac{a}{a'}(e'^2 - E'^2)\,n\,dt,
\]
and in the quotient radius, or radius vector divided by the mean distance, the secular term
\[
\left(\tfrac{3}{8}m^2 - \tfrac{81}{64}m^4\right)\frac{a}{a'}(e'^2 - E'^2)\,nt,
\]
which is, in fact, as will be shown, included implicitly in the results given in Professor Adams' Memoir.

In quoting the foregoing results, I have written $e'^2 - E'^2$ in the place of $(e' + f't)^2 - e'^2 = 2e'f't$, which in the notation of the present Memoir it should have been; and I purposely refrain from here explaining the precise signification of the symbols: this is carefully done in the sequel.

The method appears to me a very simple one in principle; and it possesses the advantage that it is not incorporated step by step with a lunar theory in which the eccentricity of the Sun's orbit is treated as constant; but it is added on to such a lunar theory, giving in the Moon's coordinates the supplementary terms which arise from the variation of the solar eccentricity, and thus serving as a verification of any process employed for taking account of such variation.

I have given the details of the work in a series of Annexes, 1 to 23: this appears to me the best course for presenting the investigation in a readable form.

\subsection*{I.}

The inclination and eccentricity of the Moon's orbit, and, \textit{a fortiori}, the variation of the position of the Ecliptic, and the Sun's latitude, are neglected; and the longitudes are measured from a fixed point in the Ecliptic.

I write $n$, the actual mean motion of the Moon at a given epoch; viz., it is assumed that the mean longitude at the time $t$ is $\epsilon + nt + n_2t^2 + \cdots$ where $\epsilon$, $n$, $n_2$, etc.\ are absolute constants; and, moreover, $a$, the calculated mean distance of the Moon; that is, $n^2a^3$ is the sum of the masses of the Earth and Moon; $a$ is therefore an absolute constant; and, in like manner, $n'$, the actual mean motion of the Sun at the same epoch, $a'$, the calculated mean distance of the Sun; that is, if it were necessary to pay attention to the secular variation of the mean motion of the Sun, the assumption would be that the mean longitude was $\epsilon' + n't + n'_2t^2 + \cdots$, $\epsilon'$, $n'$, $n'_2$, etc.\ being absolute constants, and $n'^2a'^3$ the sum of the masses of the Sun and Earth; $a'$ would thus also be an absolute constant.

But for the purpose of the present investigation the secular variation of the mean longitude of the Sun is neglected, or it is assumed that the mean longitude of the Sun is $\epsilon' + n't$, $\epsilon'$, $n'$ being absolute constants; and that $n'^2a'^3$ is the sum of the masses of the Sun and Earth, $a'$ being thus also an absolute constant.

I put also $m$, the ratio of the mean motions of the Sun and Moon; that is,
\[
m = \frac{n'}{n}, \quad \text{or } n' = mn;
\]
$m$ is also an absolute constant.

The Sun is considered as moving in an elliptic orbit, the eccentricity whereof is $e' + \delta e'$ or $e' + f't$, $e'$ and $f'$ being absolute constants; the longitude of the Sun's perigee may be taken to be $\varpi' + (1-c')n't$; so that the mean anomaly $g$ is $= n't + \epsilon' - [\varpi' + (1-c')n't] = c'n't + \epsilon' - \varpi'$; $c'$, $\epsilon'$, $\varpi'$ being absolute constants; but $c'$ is in fact treated as being $= 1$.

Hence, if $r'$, $v'$ are the radius vector and longitude of the Sun, we have
\begin{align*}
r' &= a'\,\mathrm{elqr}(e' + \delta e', g), \\
v' &= \varpi' + (1-c')n't + \mathrm{elta}(e' + \delta e', g) \\
&= n't + \epsilon' + [\mathrm{elta}(e' + \delta e', g) - g],
\end{align*}
where $g = c'n't + \text{const}$.

In the expression for the disturbing function the Sun's mass is taken to be $= n'^2a'^3$, or, what is the same thing, $= m^2n^2a'^3$.

Let $r$, $v$ be the radius vector and longitude of the Moon; then, taking the usual approximate expression of the Disturbing Function, the equations of motion are
\begin{align}
\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{dr}{dt}\right) - r\left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right)^2 + \frac{n^2a^3}{r^2} &= \frac{dR}{dr}, \\
\frac{d}{dt}\left(r^2\frac{dv}{dt}\right) &= \frac{dR}{dv}.
\end{align}

It will be convenient to assume $\rho$, the quotient radius of the Moon's orbit, $\rho'$, the quotient radius of the Sun's orbit; that is
\[
r = \rho a, \qquad r' = \rho' a'.
\]

The equations of motion thus become
\begin{align}
\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{d\rho}{dt}\right) - \rho\left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right)^2 + \frac{n^2}{\rho^2} &= m^2n^2\,\frac{dP}{d\rho}, \\
\frac{d}{dt}\left(\rho^2\frac{dv}{dt}\right) &= m^2n^2\,\frac{dP}{dv},
\end{align}
where for shortness
\[
P = \frac{\rho}{\rho'^3}\left(\tfrac{3}{2}\sin^2(v-v') - \tfrac{1}{2}\right),
\]
in which
\[
\rho' = \mathrm{elqr}(e' + \delta e', g), \quad v' = n't + \epsilon' + [\mathrm{elta}(e', g) - g].
\]

I now change the notation by writing $\rho + \delta\rho$, $v + \delta v$, in the place of $\rho$, $v$, respectively, using henceforward $\rho$, $v$ to denote
\[
\rho = \mathrm{elqr}(e', g), \quad v = n't + \epsilon' + [\mathrm{elta}(e', g) - g];
\]
and I write also $\rho + \delta\rho$, $v + \delta v$, in the place of $\rho$, $v$, using henceforward $\rho$, $v$ to denote the solutions of the equations obtained from the equations of motion by writing therein $\rho$, $v$ instead of the complete values $\rho + \delta\rho$, $v + \delta v$.

Suppose, in like manner, that the complete values of $P$, $Q$ are denoted by $P + \delta P$, $Q + \delta Q$, where
\[
\delta P = \frac{dP}{d\rho}\delta\rho + \frac{dP}{dv}\delta v + \frac{dP}{d\rho'}\delta\rho' + \frac{dP}{dv'}\delta v',
\]
with a like value for $\delta Q$, the first powers of $\delta\rho$, $\delta v$, $\delta\rho'$, $\delta v'$ being alone attended to.

Then, observing that the equations of motion are satisfied when $\delta\rho$, $\delta v$, $\delta\rho'$, $\delta v'$ are neglected, we have
\begin{align}
\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{d\delta\rho}{dt}\right) - \left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right)^2\delta\rho - 2\rho\frac{dv}{dt}\frac{d\delta v}{dt} - \frac{2n^2}{\rho^3}\delta\rho &= m^2n^2\,\delta P, \\
\frac{d}{dt}\left(\rho^2\frac{d\delta v}{dt} + 2\rho\frac{dv}{dt}\delta\rho\right) &= m^2n^2\,\delta Q.
\end{align}

The second of these equations gives
\[
\rho^2\frac{d\delta v}{dt} + 2\rho\frac{dv}{dt}\delta\rho = C + m^2n^2\int\delta Q\,dt,
\]
where the constant of integration, $C$, is to be so determined that $\delta v$ may not contain any term of the form $kt$ (for any such term is taken to be included in the term $nt$ of $v + \delta v$).

Multiplying the equation just obtained by $-\dfrac{2}{\rho}\dfrac{dv}{dt}$, and adding it to the first equation, we have
\begin{multline}
\frac{d^2\delta\rho}{dt^2} + \left(\frac{2n^2}{\rho^3} - \left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right)^2\right)\delta\rho - \frac{2}{\rho}\frac{dv}{dt}\left(C + m^2n^2\int\delta Q\,dt\right) \\
= m^2n^2\left(\delta P + \frac{2}{\rho}\frac{dv}{dt}\delta Q\right),
\end{multline}
which, with the above-mentioned integral equation, are the equations for the solution of the problem; but it will be convenient to write them under the slightly different form
\begin{align}
\frac{d^2\delta\rho}{dt^2} + n^2\delta\rho &= \left(n^2 + \frac{2n^2}{\rho^3} - \left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right)^2\right)\delta\rho + m^2n^2\left(\delta P + \frac{2}{\rho}\frac{dv}{dt}(C + \int\delta Q\,dt)\right), \\
\frac{d\delta v}{dt} &= -\frac{2}{\rho}\frac{dv}{dt}\delta\rho + \frac{1}{\rho^2}\left(C + m^2n^2\int\delta Q\,dt\right).
\end{align}

In these equations $C$ is determined, as above, by the condition that $\dfrac{d\delta v}{dt}$ may contain no constant term; the values of $\rho'$, $v'$, $\delta\rho'$, $\delta v'$ are of course given by the theory of elliptic motion, and those of $\rho$, $v$ are given by the ordinary lunar theory, in which the eccentricity of the solar orbit is treated as a constant; and then, $\delta\rho$, $\delta v$ being obtained by integrating the equations, the radius vector and longitude of the Moon are $a(\rho + \delta\rho)$ and $v + \delta v$ respectively.

We have
\[
Q = \frac{\rho}{\rho'^3}\left(-\tfrac{3}{2}\sin 2(v-v') - 2(v-v')\right).
\]

Moreover, by the lunar theory, observing that Plana's $a$ is, or may be considered, identical with the $a$ of the present Memoir, and putting also
\[
2\nu = (2-2m)nt + \text{const.}, \qquad g = c'mn't + \text{const.},
\]
we have
\begin{align}
\frac{1}{\rho} &= 1 + \tfrac{1}{6}m^2 - \tfrac{1}{3}m^2e'^2 - \tfrac{1}{4}m^2e\cos g + \tfrac{1}{8}m^2e^2\cos 2g \nonumber\\
&\quad + \tfrac{3}{8}m^2e'^2\cos 2\nu + \cdots, \\
v &= nt + \epsilon - \tfrac{3}{4}me\sin g + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2\sin 2\nu + \tfrac{11}{8}m^2e\sin(g+2\nu) \nonumber\\
&\quad - \tfrac{55}{24}m^2e\sin(2\nu-g) + \cdots,
\end{align}
where the series are carried as far as $m^2$ and $e^2$; the terms in $e'^2$ are given, as I shall have occasion to refer to them, but they are not used in the investigation, and, omitting them, the values are
\begin{align}
\frac{1}{\rho} &= 1 + \tfrac{1}{6}m^2 - \tfrac{1}{4}m^2e\cos g + \tfrac{1}{8}m^2e^2\cos 2g + \tfrac{3}{8}m^2e'^2\cos 2\nu, \\
v &= nt + \epsilon - \tfrac{3}{4}me\sin g + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2\sin 2\nu + \tfrac{11}{8}m^2e\sin(g+2\nu) \nonumber\\
&\quad - \tfrac{55}{24}m^2e\sin(2\nu-g).
\end{align}

For the coordinates of the Sun we have
\begin{align}
\frac{a'}{r'} &= 1 + e'\cos g + e'^2\cos 2g, \\
v' &= n't + \epsilon' + 2e'\sin g + \tfrac{5}{4}e'^2\sin 2g,
\end{align}
the series being carried as far as $e'^2$; but the terms in $e'^2$ are only used for the formation of $\delta\rho'$, $\delta v'$; and, omitting them, we have
\[
\frac{a'}{r'} = 1 + e'\cos g, \qquad v' = n't + \epsilon' + 2e'\sin g.
\]

If $e' + f't$ is written for $e'$, then the value of $\delta e'$ is $= f't$; but as only the terms multiplied by the simple power $f'$ are attended to, we may for convenience write $\delta e' = t$, the factor $f'$ being restored in the final results: we thus have
\begin{align*}
\delta\rho' &= -t\cos g + 2e't\cos 2g, \\
\delta v' &= -2t\sin g + \tfrac{5}{2}e't\sin 2g,
\end{align*}
and we may add the equations
\begin{align*}
\frac{dv'}{dt} &= n' + 2e'n'\cos g + \tfrac{5}{4}e'^2n'\cos 2g, \\
\delta\rho' &= -3e't - 3t\cos g + \tfrac{5}{2}e't\cos 2g, \\
\delta v' &= -2t\sin g - \tfrac{5}{4}e't\sin 2g,
\end{align*}
which will be found useful.

\subsection*{II.}

Proceeding now to the development of the solution, we have
\begin{multline}
\delta P = \frac{1}{\rho'^3}\left(1 + \tfrac{3}{2}\cos 2(v-v')\right)\delta\rho \\
+ \frac{3}{\rho'^3}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\sin 2(v-v')\right)\delta v + \frac{3}{2\rho'^3}\sin 2(v-v')\frac{\delta\rho'}{\rho'} + \cdots,
\end{multline}
where the terms containing $\delta\rho$ and $\delta v$ are (see Annex 1)
\begin{multline*}
\left(1 + \tfrac{3}{2}e'^2\right)\delta\rho + \tfrac{3}{2}e'\cos\nu\,\delta v + \tfrac{3}{2}e'\sin\nu\,\frac{\delta\rho'}{\rho'} + \cdots \\
= \left(1 + \tfrac{3}{2}e'^2\right)\left(-\tfrac{3}{4}\sin 2\nu - \tfrac{1}{2}e\sin(2\nu-g) + \tfrac{3}{4}e\sin(2\nu+g)\right) \\
+ \tfrac{3}{2}e'\cos\nu\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\cos 2\nu + e\cos(2\nu-g) - e\cos(2\nu+g)\right) + \cdots
\end{multline*}
and also
\begin{multline*}
\tfrac{3}{2}\sin 2\nu\left(-\tfrac{3}{4}\sin 2\nu + \cdots\right) + \tfrac{3}{2}\cos 2\nu\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\cos 2\nu + \cdots\right) \\
= \tfrac{3}{4}\left(-\tfrac{3}{2}\sin^2 2\nu + \cos^2 2\nu\right) + \cdots
\end{multline*}
where the terms containing $\delta\rho'$ and $\delta v'$ are (see Annex 2)
\begin{multline*}
\tfrac{3}{2}\sin 2\nu\left(-t\cos g + 2e't\cos 2g\right) \\
+ \tfrac{3}{2}\cos 2\nu\left(-2t\sin g + \tfrac{5}{2}e't\sin 2g\right) + \cdots
\end{multline*}

The development being continued through the annexes referred to, the final results are obtained for the secular acceleration of the Moon's mean motion, confirming those of Professor Adams.
\clearpage


% =========================================================
% Chunk: cayley_vol03_pages_551_594.tex  (orig pages 551--594)
% =========================================================
%=======================================================================
% PAPER 221
%=======================================================================
\markboth{221.\quad ON THE SECULAR ACCELERATION OF THE MOON'S MEAN MOTION.}{}

\section*{\large 221.}
\subsection*{ON THE SECULAR ACCELERATION OF THE MOON'S MEAN MOTION.}

\begin{center}
[From the \textit{Monthly Notices of the Royal Astronomical Society}, vol.~\textsc{xxii}. (1862), pp. 171--186.]
\end{center}

\medskip

The values of the certain terms in the expressions of the moon's coordinates,
which give the secular acceleration of the moon's mean motion, were originally
calculated by me by a direct and elementary method, which (as it appears to me)
has the advantage over Plana's very elegant process, that in it we have not to
assume without proof that the required acceleration arises entirely from the
variation of the eccentricity of the earth's orbit. The solution was originally
obtained in a literal form; and the numerical results were given to a greater
degree of accuracy than was requisite. I propose in the present paper to
present the investigation under a somewhat altered form; and to give the results
in the numerical form only, but to a greater degree of accuracy than was
attained in my original memoir. The following changes have been made; viz.
instead of the disturbing function
\[
\frac{m'}{\rho'^3}\left(x'x + y'y + z'z\right)
\]
I employ the equivalent form
\[
\frac{m'}{\rho'^3}\rho^2\cos\angle\left(\rho, \rho'\right),
\]
and in connexion herewith, I introduce the coordinates $P$, $Q$ (functions of
$\rho$, $v$) employed by Mr Delaunay in his new lunar theory; viz. writing
\[
P = \frac{\rho}{r}\cos(v - nt - \epsilon) = \cos\phi\cos(v - nt - \epsilon),\quad
Q = \frac{\rho}{r}\sin(v - nt - \epsilon) = \cos\phi\sin(v - nt - \epsilon),
\]
so that $P$, $Q$ are the values of $\cos(v - nt - \epsilon)$, $\sin(v - nt - \epsilon)$,
multiplied by the cosine of the moon's latitude; and the disturbing function
is
\[
m'\frac{\rho^2}{\rho'^3}\left(P\cos v' + Q\sin v'\right).
\]
The expressions for $\delta P$, $\delta Q$ are obtained in a more simple form by
assuming at once the numerical value of the ratio $m$ of the mean motions.
I write also $t = nt$, and take $\tau$, $g$ instead of Delaunay's $\xi$, $g$; viz.
$\tau$ is the moon's mean longitude minus the longitude of the perigee; and
$g$ is the longitude of the moon's perigee minus that of the sun's perigee.
The epoch is taken to be the time of the sun's perigee passing the node; so
that at the epoch $\tau = \varpi - \theta$, $g = \varpi - \varpi'$; where
$\varpi$, $\varpi'$ are the longitudes of the moon's perigee and the sun's
perigee, and $\theta$ is the longitude of the node. This being so, the mean
longitude of the moon is $nt + \epsilon + v_1$, where $v_1$ is the value of
$v$ at the epoch; the mean longitude of the moon's perigee is $nt + \epsilon + v_1 - \tau$;
and the mean longitude of the sun's perigee is $nt + \epsilon + v_1 - \tau - g$.
The sun's mean anomaly is $n't + \epsilon' - (nt + \epsilon + v_1 - \tau - g)$;
and introducing the numerical value $n' = (1 - m)n$, this is
$=(1 - m)nt + \epsilon' - nt - \epsilon - v_1 + \tau + g$, $= -mnt + \epsilon' - \epsilon - v_1 + \tau + g$,
which at the epoch vanishes: we have therefore $\epsilon' = \epsilon + v_1 - \tau - g$,
and the mean anomaly is $= -mnt + \tau + g$, $= -t + \tau + g$, writing therein
$t$ for $mnt$; that is, it is $-(t - \tau - g)$. Hence writing $g' = t - \tau - g$,
which is the sun's mean anomaly, then $g = t - \tau - g'$; and the arguments
$2\tau$, $2\tau - g$, $2\tau + g$, \&c., become $2\tau$, $2\tau + g' - t$, $3t - 2\tau - g'$;
or, as the terms containing $t$ need not be exhibited, these may be written
$2\tau$, $2\tau - g'$, $-2\tau + g'$, that is $2\tau$, $2\tau - g'$, $2\tau + g'$,
the sign only of the sine or cosine terms being attended to. And so in other
cases; the arguments are all given in terms of $\tau$ and $g'$; but the accented
letter $g'$ will for convenience be written without the accent.

\mypar
I.

\mypar
We have
\[
\frac{d^2\rho}{dt^2} - \rho\left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right)^2 + \frac{n^2a^3}{\rho^2} = n^2\delta R,\qquad
\frac{d(\rho^2\frac{dv}{dt})}{dt} = n^2\delta S;
\]
and thence
\[
\frac{d^2\delta\rho}{dt^2} - 2\rho n\delta\left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right) - n^2\delta\rho + \frac{2n^2a^3}{\rho^3}\delta\rho
= n^2\delta R,
\]
that is
\[
\frac{d^2\delta\rho}{dt^2} + n^2\delta\rho - 2n^2\delta\left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right) = n^2\delta R,
\]
if for the undisturbed values we write $\rho = a$, $\dfrac{dv}{dt} = n$;
and
\[
\frac{d\delta v}{dt} = \frac{2n}{\rho}\delta\rho + \frac{n}{\rho^2}\delta S
= 2n\delta\rho + n\delta S,
\]
if for the undisturbed values $\rho = 1$, $\dfrac{dv}{dt} = n$, or say
$\rho = 1$, $dv = n\,dt$, or simply $dv = dt$, taking $n = 1$. We thus
have
\[
\frac{d^2\delta\rho}{dt^2} + \delta\rho - 2\delta\left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right) = \delta R,\qquad
\frac{d\delta v}{dt} = 2\delta\rho + \delta S;
\]
or, what is the same thing,
\[
\frac{d^2\delta\rho}{dt^2} + \delta\rho = \delta P,\qquad
\frac{d\delta v}{dt} = \delta Q,
\]
if for shortness
\[
\delta P = \delta R + 2\int\delta Q\,dt,\qquad
\delta Q = 2\delta\rho + \delta S;
\]
so that, as regards the terms in question, the original equations serve to
determine $\delta\rho$, $\delta v$.

\mypar
II.

\mypar
The value of the disturbing function is
\[
\delta R = \frac{m'}{\rho'^3}\rho^2\left(P\cos v' + Q\sin v'\right),
\]
where
\[
P = \frac{\rho}{r}\cos(v - t - \epsilon),\qquad
Q = \frac{\rho}{r}\sin(v - t - \epsilon).
\]
We require the non-periodic part of $\dfrac{d\delta v}{dt}$, that is of
$2\delta\rho + \delta S$; and in forming this we may in $\delta\rho$, $\delta S$
take account of the periodic terms only, neglecting the terms which depend
on $t$ (secular terms) and the constant terms; these last, in fact, give
only periodic terms in $\delta\rho$, $\delta S$. In $\delta R$, the term
$\dfrac{m'}{\rho'^3}\rho^2P\cos v'$ gives in $\delta P$, and thence in
$\delta\rho$, terms involving $\cos g'$ with the factor $e'$ (the sun's
eccentricity); and the term $\dfrac{m'}{\rho'^3}\rho^2Q\sin v'$ gives in
$\delta Q$, and thence in $\delta v$, terms involving $\sin g'$ with the
factor $e'$. The corresponding terms in $\delta\rho$, $\delta v$ give in
$\delta P$ ($= \delta R + 2\int\delta Q\,dt$) and in $\delta Q$
($= 2\delta\rho + \delta S$) the terms which produce the secular acceleration.
And so in other cases; we require in $\delta P$ the terms
\[
A + Bt\cos g' + Ct\sin g',
\]
and in $\delta Q$ the terms
\[
A' + B't\sin g' + C't\cos g',
\]
where the constant terms $A$, $A'$ are introduced for the reason above;
the terms in question give in $\delta\rho$ the terms
\[
A + \frac{1}{2}Bt\cos g' + \frac{1}{2}Ct\sin g',
\]
and in $\delta v$ the terms
\[
A't + \frac{1}{2}B't^2\sin g' - \frac{1}{2}C't^2\cos g' - \frac{1}{2}B'\frac{\cos g'}{m^2}
+ \frac{1}{2}C'\frac{\sin g'}{m^2};
\]
where the constant terms $\dfrac{1}{2}B'\dfrac{\cos g'}{m^2}$, $-\dfrac{1}{2}C'\dfrac{\sin g'}{m^2}$
are written down so as to make the whole values of $\delta\rho$, $\delta v$
correct as far as the terms in question; and from them we have in
$\dfrac{d\delta v}{dt}$, the secular term $A't$, that is $-2B't$, if
$A' = -2B$ (for observe that in $\delta Q$ the non-periodic part is $A' + B't\sin g' + C't\cos g'$,
where $A' = -2B$, and the value of $\dfrac{d\delta v}{dt}$ is $A' - \dfrac{2B'}{m^2} + \&\mathrm{c}.$,
which in regard to the constant term gives $A' - \dfrac{2B'}{m^2} = -2B - \dfrac{2B'}{m^2}$,
$= -2B$ since $B'$ is a term of the order $m^2$).

\mypar
III.

\mypar
We require the expression for $\dfrac{m'}{\rho'^3}$, say
\[
\frac{m'}{\rho'^3} = m^2n^2\left(1 + \frac{3}{2}e'\cos g' + \&\mathrm{c}.\right),
\]
and then, by what precedes, in forming the values of $\delta P$, $\delta Q$,
it is sufficient to take
\[
\frac{m'}{\rho'^3} = m^2n^2\left(1 + \frac{3}{2}e'\cos g'\right).
\]
In the expressions for $P$, $Q$ we require only the non-periodic terms and
the terms containing the argument $g'$, $2g'$. Writing
\[
\frac{\rho}{r} = 1 + \frac{1}{6}m^2 - e\cos\tau - \frac{1}{6}m^2\cos 2\tau,\qquad
v - t - \epsilon = 2e\sin\tau + \frac{5}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau + \frac{1}{2}m^2\sin(2\tau - 2g'),
\]
where in $v - t - \epsilon$ the term $\frac{1}{2}m^2\sin(2\tau - 2g')$ arises
from the evection, and is in fact $\frac{1}{2}m^2\sin(2\tau - 2g')$; we find
\begin{align*}
P &= \left(1 + \tfrac{1}{6}m^2\right)\cos(v - t - \epsilon)
 - e\cos\tau\cos(v - t - \epsilon)
 - \tfrac{1}{6}m^2\cos 2\tau\cos(v - t - \epsilon),\\
Q &= \left(1 + \tfrac{1}{6}m^2\right)\sin(v - t - \epsilon)
 - e\cos\tau\sin(v - t - \epsilon)
 - \tfrac{1}{6}m^2\cos 2\tau\sin(v - t - \epsilon).
\end{align*}

\mypar
But
\[
\cos(v - t - \epsilon) = 1 - 2e^2\sin^2\tau + \&\mathrm{c}.
= 1 - e^2 + e^2\cos 2\tau,\qquad
\sin(v - t - \epsilon) = 2e\sin\tau + \tfrac{5}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau;
\]
and hence, up to the required order,
\begin{align*}
P &= \left(1 + \tfrac{1}{6}m^2 - e^2\right) + \left(-e + 2e\right)\cos\tau
 + \left(\tfrac{1}{6}m^2 + e^2\right)\cos 2\tau
 = 1 + \tfrac{1}{6}m^2 - e^2 + e\cos\tau + \left(\tfrac{1}{6}m^2 + e^2\right)\cos 2\tau,\\
Q &= \left(-e + 2e\right)\sin\tau + \tfrac{5}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau
 = e\sin\tau + \tfrac{5}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau;
\end{align*}
where, however, in the term $e\cos\tau$, $e\sin\tau$, we must restore the
evection, that is write
\[
\tau + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2\sin(2\tau - 2g')\quad\text{for}\quad \tau;
\]
so that the term $e\cos\tau$ is replaced by
\[
e\cos\tau - \tfrac{1}{2}m^2e\sin\tau\sin(2\tau - 2g')
= e\cos\tau + \tfrac{1}{4}m^2e\cos(3\tau - 2g') - \tfrac{1}{4}m^2e\cos(\tau + 2g'),
\]
and the term $e\sin\tau$ is replaced by
\[
e\sin\tau + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2e\cos\tau\sin(2\tau - 2g')
= e\sin\tau + \tfrac{1}{4}m^2e\sin(3\tau - 2g') + \tfrac{1}{4}m^2e\sin(\tau + 2g').
\]
We thus have
\begin{align*}
P &= 1 + \tfrac{1}{6}m^2 - e^2 + e\cos\tau + \left(\tfrac{1}{6}m^2 + e^2\right)\cos 2\tau\\
&\quad + \tfrac{1}{4}m^2e\cos(3\tau - 2g') - \tfrac{1}{4}m^2e\cos(\tau + 2g'),\\
Q &= e\sin\tau + \tfrac{5}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau + \tfrac{1}{4}m^2e\sin(3\tau - 2g')
 + \tfrac{1}{4}m^2e\sin(\tau + 2g').
\end{align*}
Or, restoring in the terms $\frac{1}{4}m^2e\cos(3\tau - 2g')$ and
$\frac{1}{4}m^2e\sin(3\tau - 2g')$, the argument $g$ instead of $g'$,
that is $2\tau + g$ instead of $2\tau - 2g'$, or say $2\tau + g'$ instead of
$2\tau - 2g'$ (since the difference affects only periodic terms of higher
orders), and similarly in the other terms, we have
\begin{align*}
P &= 1 + \tfrac{1}{6}m^2 - e^2 + e\cos\tau + \left(\tfrac{1}{6}m^2 + e^2\right)\cos 2\tau\\
&\quad + \tfrac{1}{4}m^2e\cos(2\tau + g') - \tfrac{1}{4}m^2e\cos(\tau + 2g'),\\
Q &= e\sin\tau + \tfrac{5}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau + \tfrac{1}{4}m^2e\sin(2\tau + g')
 + \tfrac{1}{4}m^2e\sin(\tau + 2g'),
\end{align*}
which, in fact, are the expressions made use of by Mr Delaunay.

\mypar
But for the present purpose, retaining the argument $g'$ only in those terms
which contain the factor $e'$, it is sufficient to write
\[
P = 1 + \tfrac{1}{6}m^2 - e^2 + e\cos\tau + \left(\tfrac{1}{6}m^2 + e^2\right)\cos 2\tau,\qquad
Q = e\sin\tau + \tfrac{5}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau.
\]

\mypar
Moreover
\[
\rho^2 = r^2 = 1 + \tfrac{1}{3}m^2 - 2e\cos\tau + e^2\left(-1 + \tfrac{5}{2}\cos 2\tau\right),
\]
and we thence have
\[
\frac{m'}{\rho'^3}\rho^2 = m^2\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{3}m^2 - 2e\cos\tau + \tfrac{3}{2}e'\cos g'
 + e^2\left(-1 + \tfrac{5}{2}\cos 2\tau\right)\right).
\]

\mypar
We have moreover $\cos v' = \cos(t - g')$, $\sin v' = \sin(t - g')$; or, since
the argument $t$ may be omitted, $\cos v' = \cos g'$, $\sin v' = -\sin g'$.
Hence
\begin{align*}
\delta R &= m^2\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{3}m^2 - 2e\cos\tau + \tfrac{3}{2}e'\cos g'
 + e^2\left(-1 + \tfrac{5}{2}\cos 2\tau\right)\right)\\
&\qquad \times P\cos g',
\end{align*}
\begin{align*}
\delta S &= m^2\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{3}m^2 - 2e\cos\tau + \tfrac{3}{2}e'\cos g'
 + e^2\left(-1 + \tfrac{5}{2}\cos 2\tau\right)\right)\\
&\qquad \times Q(-\sin g').
\end{align*}

\mypar
IV.

\mypar
In forming $\delta P$ ($= \delta R + 2\int\delta Q\,dt$), the periodic terms of
$\delta Q$ give rise to the secular terms in $\delta\rho$; but they also give
rise to periodic terms which must be taken into account in forming the value
of $\delta Q$. We have
\[
\delta Q = 2\delta\rho + \delta S,
\]
and the periodic terms of $\delta\rho$ must be combined with $\delta S$.

\mypar
The investigation is hence somewhat complicated; but it may be presented under
a more simple form by the following consideration. We ultimately require in
$\delta P$ the non-periodic terms, the terms in $t\cos g'$, and the terms in
$t\sin g'$; and in $\delta Q$ the non-periodic terms, the terms in $t\sin g'$,
and the terms in $t\cos g'$. Let $\delta P_0$, $\delta Q_0$ denote the values
which $\delta P$, $\delta Q$ would have if in the expression for $\delta Q$ we
were to omit the term $2\delta\rho$, and write $\delta Q = \delta S$; that is,
let $\delta P_0 = \delta R + 2\int\delta Q_0\,dt$, $\delta Q_0 = \delta S$; then
$\delta P_0$, $\delta Q_0$ can be at once obtained. We have $\delta P = \delta P_0$
(terms of order $m^2$ and $m^2e'$ in $\delta P$ are unaltered), but the terms of
order $m^2e'$ in $\delta P$ are altered by the substitution for $\delta\rho$ (in
$\delta Q = 2\delta\rho + \delta S$) of the terms of the orders $m^2$ and $m^2e'$.

\mypar
The terms of order $m^2$ in $\delta\rho$ are obtained from $\delta P_0$, by simply
dividing by $-3$; and then in $\delta Q$ we have the term $2\delta\rho$; that is,
$\delta Q = \delta S + 2\delta\rho$. But $\delta S$ contains no term of the order
$m^2$; hence in $\delta Q$, as regards terms of this order, $\delta Q = 2\delta\rho$;
and the terms of order $m^2$ in $\delta Q$ are equal to those of order $m^2$ in
$2\delta\rho$, that is, in $2(-\frac{1}{3}\delta P_0) = -\frac{2}{3}\delta P_0$.
These terms are periodic; and in the expression for $\delta P$, we have
$\delta P = \delta P_0 + 2\int\delta Q\,dt$; that is, the terms of order $m^2$ in
$\delta P$ are unaltered; but we have in $\delta Q$ a term $-\frac{2}{3}\delta P_0$,
which, combined with the terms of order $m^2e'$ in $\delta R$, gives in $\delta P$
terms of order $m^2e'$.

\mypar
V.

\mypar
Writing for shortness
\[
\Theta = \frac{m'}{\rho'^3}\rho^2,
\]
we have
\[
\delta R = \Theta \cdot P\cos g',\qquad
\delta S = \Theta \cdot Q(-\sin g'),
\]
and thence
\[
\delta P_0 = \delta R + 2\int\delta Q_0\,dt
= \Theta P\cos g' + 2\int\Theta(-Q\sin g')\,dt.
\]

\mypar
But
\[
\Theta = m^2\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{3}m^2 - 2e\cos\tau + \tfrac{3}{2}e'\cos g'
 + e^2\left(-1 + \tfrac{5}{2}\cos 2\tau\right)\right).
\]

\mypar
For the terms of order $m^2$, we have $\Theta = m^2$, and thence
\[
\delta P_0 = m^2\cos g',\qquad
\delta Q_0 = -m^2\sin g' \cdot Q;
\]
so that, omitting the arguments which do not contain $g'$,
\begin{align*}
\delta P_0 &= m^2\cos g' \cdot e\cos\tau = \tfrac{1}{2}m^2e\cos g' \cdot \cos\tau,\\
\delta Q_0 &= -m^2\sin g' \cdot e\sin\tau = -\tfrac{1}{2}m^2e\sin g' \cdot \sin\tau.
\end{align*}
These give in $\delta\rho$ the terms
\[
\delta\rho = -\tfrac{1}{6}m^2e\cos g' \cdot \cos\tau,
\]
and in $\delta Q$ the term
\[
2\delta\rho = -\tfrac{1}{3}m^2e\cos g' \cdot \cos\tau.
\]

\mypar
For the terms of order $m^2e'$, we have in $\Theta$ the term $\frac{3}{2}m^2e'\cos g'$;
and the terms of order $m^2$ in $P$ and $Q$ give in $\delta P$ a term
\[
\tfrac{3}{2}m^2e'\cos^2g' = \tfrac{3}{4}m^2e',
\]
which is non-periodic; and in $\delta Q$ a term
\[
-\tfrac{3}{2}m^2e'\cos g'\sin g' = -\tfrac{3}{4}m^2e'\sin 2g',
\]
which is periodic. Hence in $\delta P$ we have the non-periodic term
$\frac{3}{4}m^2e'$; and in $\delta Q$ we have the periodic term
$-\frac{3}{4}m^2e'\sin 2g'$.

\mypar
But we have also in $\delta P$ the term arising from $2\int\delta Q\,dt$, where
$\delta Q$ is the term $-\frac{1}{3}m^2e\cos g' \cdot \cos\tau$, which, combined
with the term in $\Theta$, gives
\[
-\tfrac{1}{3}m^2e\cos g' \cdot \cos\tau \times \tfrac{3}{2}m^2e'\cos g'
= -\tfrac{1}{2}m^4ee'\cos^2g' \cdot \cos\tau
= -\tfrac{1}{4}m^4ee'\cos\tau,
\]
which is non-periodic. Hence we have the non-periodic term $-\frac{1}{4}m^4ee'\cos\tau$;
which, integrated in $2\int\delta Q\,dt$, gives the non-periodic term
$-\frac{1}{2}m^4ee't\cos\tau$. But this is a term which would ultimately give
in $\delta\rho$ the term $-\frac{1}{6}m^4ee't\cos\tau$; and this is of the order
$m^4e$, which is beyond the order we are seeking.

\mypar
We have in $\delta P$ the term arising from $2\int\delta Q\,dt$, where $\delta Q$
is the periodic term $-\frac{3}{4}m^2e'\sin 2g'$. This gives in $\delta P$ the term
\[
2\int\left(-\tfrac{3}{4}m^2e'\sin 2g'\right)dt
= \tfrac{3}{4}m^2e'\frac{\cos 2g'}{m} = \tfrac{3}{4}m e'\cos 2g',
\]
which is periodic, and does not affect the secular acceleration.

\mypar
The terms of order $m^2e'$ in $\delta P$ are thus
\[
\tfrac{3}{4}m^2e' + \tfrac{3}{2}m^2e'\cos g' \cdot e\cos\tau,
\]
and those in $\delta Q$ are
\[
-\tfrac{3}{2}m^2e'\sin g' \cdot e\sin\tau.
\]

\mypar
Combining these results, we have
\begin{align*}
\delta P &= \tfrac{3}{4}m^2e' + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2e\cos g' \cdot \cos\tau
 + \tfrac{3}{2}m^2e'\cos g' \cdot e\cos\tau,\\
\delta Q &= -\tfrac{1}{2}m^2e\sin g' \cdot \sin\tau
 - \tfrac{3}{2}m^2e'\sin g' \cdot e\sin\tau.
\end{align*}

\mypar
VI.

\mypar
From $\delta P$ and $\delta Q$ we obtain $\delta\rho$ and $\delta v$; and thence
$\delta P$ and $\delta Q$, the complete values. We have
\[
\delta\rho = \frac{1}{\frac{d^2}{dt^2} + 1}\delta P,\qquad
\delta v = \int\delta Q\,dt.
\]

\mypar
For the terms of order $m^2$, we have
\[
\delta P = \tfrac{1}{2}m^2e\cos g' \cdot \cos\tau,
\qquad
\delta Q = -\tfrac{1}{2}m^2e\sin g' \cdot \sin\tau;
\]
and thence
\[
\delta\rho = -\tfrac{1}{2}m^2e\cos g' \cdot \cos\tau,
\qquad
\delta v = \tfrac{1}{2}m^2e\sin g' \cdot \cos\tau.
\]

\mypar
But we must also take into account the terms of order $m^2e'$ in $\delta P$, $\delta Q$.
We have
\[
\delta P = \tfrac{3}{4}m^2e' + \tfrac{3}{2}m^2e'\cos g' \cdot e\cos\tau,
\qquad
\delta Q = -\tfrac{3}{2}m^2e'\sin g' \cdot e\sin\tau.
\]

\mypar
The non-periodic term $\frac{3}{4}m^2e'$ gives in $\delta\rho$ the non-periodic term
$\frac{3}{4}m^2e'$; and in $\delta v$ the term $\frac{3}{4}m^2e't$.

\mypar
The term $\frac{3}{2}m^2e'\cos g' \cdot e\cos\tau$ gives in $\delta\rho$ the term
\[
\tfrac{3}{2}m^2e'\cos g' \cdot \frac{e\cos\tau}{0} = \tfrac{3}{2}m^2e'\cos g' \cdot (-e\cos\tau) = -\tfrac{3}{2}m^2ee'\cos g' \cdot \cos\tau;
\]
that is, since $\frac{1}{\frac{d^2}{dt^2}+1}\cos\tau = \frac{\cos\tau}{-1+1}$, which is infinite,
we must write the equation as $\left(\frac{d^2}{dt^2}+1\right)\delta\rho = \delta P$; or
if $\delta P = A\cos\tau + B\sin\tau$, then $\delta\rho = \frac{1}{2}(A t\sin\tau - B t\cos\tau)$.

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 529 (PDF page 551) -- continuing with detailed calculations
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
\markboth{221.\quad ON THE SECULAR ACCELERATION\ \ldots}{}

\mypar
The calculation is continued in the Annexes. We have
\begin{align*}
\delta P &= \tfrac{3}{2}e' \cdot t\cos g' - \tfrac{15}{4}e' \quad \text{,, } 2\tau\\
&\quad + \tfrac{21}{4} \quad \text{,, } 2\tau - g'
 - \tfrac{3}{4} \quad \text{,, } 2\tau + g'\\
&\quad + \tfrac{9}{2}e' \quad \text{,, } 2g'
 + \tfrac{11}{2}e' \quad \text{,, } 2\tau - 2g',
\end{align*}
and similarly (see Annex~5),
\begin{align*}
\delta Q &= \tfrac{15}{2}e' \cdot t\sin 2\tau\\
&\quad - \tfrac{21}{4} \quad \text{,, } 2\tau - g'
 + \tfrac{3}{4} \quad \text{,, } 2\tau + g'\\
&\quad - \tfrac{11}{2}e' \quad \text{,, } 2\tau - 2g'.
\end{align*}

\mypar
But in the foregoing expression of $\delta P$ the terms belonging to the arguments
$g'$, $2g'$ give in $\delta\rho$, terms which rise by integration in $\delta v$;
and in forming the expressions for $\delta P$, $\delta Q$, it is proper to take
account of these terms. Attending only to the terms in question, we have
\[
\frac{d^2\delta\rho}{dt^2} + \delta\rho = m^2n^2\delta P
= \tfrac{3}{2}m^2 \cdot t\cos g' + \tfrac{9}{2}m^2e' \quad \text{,, } 2g'.
\]

\mypar
Now in general, if
\[
\frac{d^2\delta\rho}{dt^2} + n^2\delta\rho = n^2 \cdot t\cos n\alpha t,
\]
then
\[
\delta\rho = \frac{2\alpha}{(1-\alpha^2)^2}\sin n\alpha t + \frac{1}{1-\alpha^2} \cdot t\cos n\alpha t;
\]
and hence the foregoing equation gives in $\delta\rho$ the terms
\[
\begin{array}{r@{}l@{}l}
3m^2 & \sin g' & + \tfrac{3}{2} \cdot t\cos g'\\
+ 18m^2e' & \text{,, } 2g' & + \tfrac{9}{2}m^2e' \quad \text{,, } 2g';
\end{array}
\]
or, neglecting the terms which contain $m^3$, the terms of $\delta\rho$ are
\[
3m^2 \sin g' + \tfrac{3}{2} \cdot t\cos g' + 18m^2e' \sin 2g' + \tfrac{9}{2}m^2e' \cdot t\cos 2g'.
\]
Substituting these terms in
\[
n \times \frac{d\delta v}{dt} - 2n\delta\rho = 3m^2 \cdot t\cos g' - 9m^2e' \cdot t\sin 2g',
\]
and since, in general,
\[
\int t\cos n\alpha t \cdot dt = \frac{1}{n^2\alpha^2}\cos n\alpha t + \frac{1}{n\alpha} \cdot t\sin n\alpha t,
\]
we obtain in $\delta v$ the terms
\[
\tfrac{3}{2}\cos g' + \tfrac{3}{4}m^2 \cdot t\sin g'
- \tfrac{1}{4}m^2 \cdot \tfrac{9}{2}e' \cdot t\sin 2g',
\]
or for the present purpose the terms
\[
\tfrac{3}{2}\cos g' + \tfrac{3}{4}m^2 \cdot t\sin g';
\]
and these give in $\delta P$ the additional terms (see Annex~6)
\[
\tfrac{3}{2}\sin 2\tau + \tfrac{3}{4}m^2 \cdot \text{,, } 2\tau - g',
\]
and in $\delta Q$ the additional terms (see Annex~7)
\[
\tfrac{3}{4}m^2 \cdot \cos 2\tau + \tfrac{3}{4}m^2 \cdot \text{,, } 2\tau - g'
+ \tfrac{3}{4}m^2 \cdot \text{,, } 2\tau + g'.
\]

\mypar
Combining the foregoing results, we have
\begin{align*}
\delta P &= \tfrac{3}{2}e' \cdot t\cos g' - \tfrac{15}{4}e' \quad \text{,, } 2\tau
 + \tfrac{21}{4} \quad \text{,, } 2\tau - g'
 - \tfrac{3}{4} \quad \text{,, } 2\tau + g'\\
&\quad + \tfrac{9}{2}e' \quad \text{,, } 2g'
 + \tfrac{11}{2}e' \quad \text{,, } 2\tau - 2g'
 + \tfrac{3}{2} \quad \text{,, } 2\tau
 + \tfrac{3}{4}m^2 \quad \text{,, } 2\tau - g',
\end{align*}
and
\begin{align*}
\delta Q &= \tfrac{15}{2}e' \cdot t\sin 2\tau
 - \tfrac{21}{4} \quad \text{,, } 2\tau - g'
 + \tfrac{3}{4} \quad \text{,, } 2\tau + g'\\
&\quad - \tfrac{11}{2}e' \quad \text{,, } 2\tau - 2g'
 + \tfrac{3}{4}m^2 \quad \text{,, } 2\tau
 + \tfrac{3}{4}m^2 \quad \text{,, } 2\tau - g'
 + \tfrac{3}{4}m^2 \quad \text{,, } 2\tau + g';
\end{align*}
whence also (see Annex~8)
\[
n \times \delta\rho = \tfrac{3}{2}e' \cdot t\cos g' - \tfrac{15}{4}e' \cdot \text{,, } 2\tau
+ \&\mathrm{c}.
\]

\mypar
The equation for $\delta\rho$ may be written,
\[
\frac{d^2\delta\rho}{dt^2} + n^2\delta\rho = n^2\delta P,
\]
and the solution is
\[
\delta\rho = A\cos(nt + B) + \frac{1}{n}\int_0^t n^2\delta P \cdot \sin n(t - t')\,dt'.
\]
Taking the constant term and the terms in $t\cos g'$, $t\sin g'$, we have
\[
\delta\rho = -\frac{1}{3}\delta P_0 + \frac{1}{2}t\sin t \cdot (\text{coeff. of }\cos t\text{ in }\delta P)
- \frac{1}{2}t\cos t \cdot (\text{coeff. of }\sin t\text{ in }\delta P);
\]
and thence
\[
\delta\rho = \tfrac{3}{2}e' \cdot t\cos g' - \tfrac{15}{4}e' \quad \text{,, } 2\tau
+ \&\mathrm{c}.
\]

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 530--531 (PDF pages 552--553)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------

\mypar
In the foregoing values of $\delta P$, $\delta Q$, the arguments are given in
terms of $\tau$ and $g'$, but for the numerical calculation it is more
convenient to use the arguments in terms of the mean longitudes. Restoring
the argument $t$ in the coefficients of $e'$, and writing $2\tau = 2nt - 2g'$
(or, what is the same thing, omitting $t$ and restoring $g'$), we have
\begin{align*}
\delta P &= \left(\tfrac{3}{2} - \tfrac{15}{4} + \tfrac{21}{4} - \tfrac{3}{4}\right)e'\cos g'\\
&\quad + \left(\tfrac{9}{2} - \tfrac{11}{2} + \tfrac{3}{2}\right)e'\cos(2\tau - g')\\
&\quad + \&\mathrm{c}.\\
&= \left(\tfrac{3}{2} - \tfrac{15}{4} + \tfrac{21}{4} - \tfrac{3}{4}\right)e'\cos g'
+ \&\mathrm{c}.\\
&= \tfrac{3}{2}e'\cos g' + \&\mathrm{c}.
\end{align*}

\mypar
VII.

\mypar
The secular acceleration is obtained from the non-periodic part of
$\dfrac{d\delta v}{dt}$, that is of $2\delta\rho + \delta S$. We have
\[
\frac{d\delta v}{dt} = 2\delta\rho + \delta S,
\]
and the secular term is
\[
-2 \times \frac{1}{3} \times \text{(non-periodic part of }\delta P\text{)}.
\]

\mypar
But we have in $\delta P$ the terms
\[
\tfrac{3}{4}m^2e' + \tfrac{3}{2}m^2e'\cos g' \cdot e\cos\tau,
\]
and the non-periodic part of this is
\[
\tfrac{3}{4}m^2e';
\]
and thence the secular term in $\dfrac{d\delta v}{dt}$ is
\[
-2 \times \tfrac{1}{3} \times \tfrac{3}{4}m^2e' = -\tfrac{1}{2}m^2e'.
\]
But this is not the whole; for we have to consider the terms arising from
the combination of the periodic terms in $\delta\rho$ and $\delta S$.

\mypar
The expression for $\delta S$ is
\[
\delta S = \Theta \cdot Q(-\sin g'),
\]
and in this we have the term
\[
-2e\cos\tau \times e\sin\tau \times (-\sin g') = e^2\sin 2\tau \cdot \sin g'.
\]
This gives in combination with the periodic term in $\delta\rho$, the non-periodic
terms which contribute to the secular acceleration.

\mypar
The complete calculation is given in the Annexes. The result is that the
coefficient of the secular acceleration is
\[
-\left(\tfrac{3}{2}m^2 - \tfrac{3771}{64}m^4\right) \int (e'^2 - E'^2)\,n\,dt,
\]
or, as it is written by Prof. Adams,
\[
-\left(\tfrac{3}{2}m^2 - \tfrac{2187}{128}m^4\right) \int (e'^2 - E'^2)\,n\,dt.
\]
The difference between these two expressions is
\[
\left(\tfrac{3771}{64} - \tfrac{2187}{128}\right)m^4 = \tfrac{7542 - 2187}{128}m^4 = \tfrac{5355}{128}m^4,
\]
and it is in fact by the addition of these that Plana's coefficient
$\frac{2187}{128}$ is changed into $\frac{3771}{64}$.

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 532--533 (PDF pages 554--555)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
\markboth{221.\quad ON THE SECULAR ACCELERATION\ \ldots}{}

\mypar
The value of the coefficient is
\[
\tfrac{3}{2}m^2 - \tfrac{3771}{64}m^4;
\]
and it is interesting to see how this is made up. Writing
\[
M = \tfrac{3}{2}m^2,\qquad N = \tfrac{3771}{64}m^4,
\]
we have
\[
\text{Coefficient} = M - N.
\]

\mypar
In the several Annexes the terms are calculated as follows:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r@{.}l}
\toprule
\multicolumn{2}{c}{\textit{Value.}} \\
\midrule
$M$ & $= \frac{3}{2}m^2$ \\
$M_1$ & $= \frac{15}{4}m^2$ \\
$M_2$ & $= \frac{21}{4}m^2$ \\
$M_3$ & $= \frac{3}{4}m^2$ \\
$M_4$ & $= \frac{9}{2}m^2$ \\
$M_5$ & $= \frac{11}{2}m^2$ \\
$M_6$ & $= \frac{3}{2}m^2$ \\
$M_7$ & $= \frac{3}{4}m^2$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\mypar
The combination of these terms gives the final result; and it will be observed
that the terms which contain $e'$ are those which ultimately give the secular
acceleration.

\mypar
The calculation is completed in Annex~23; and the result is
\[
-\tfrac{1275}{32}m^4ne't,
\]
which is the required coefficient of the secular acceleration.

\mypar
It may be remarked that the terms which rise by integration in $\delta v$ are
those which contain the argument $g'$ (or $2g'$) in the coefficient; and these
terms give the secular acceleration. The terms which contain $t$ in the coefficient
give the secular variation of the mean motion; and the constant terms give the
periodic variations.

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 534--535 (PDF pages 556--557)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------

\mypar
IV.

\mypar
The part $\dfrac{m'}{\rho'^3}\rho^2P\cos g'$ of $\delta R$ gives
\[
\frac{m'}{\rho'^3}\rho^2 = m^2\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{3}m^2 - 2e\cos\tau + \tfrac{3}{2}e'\cos g'
+ e^2\left(-1 + \tfrac{5}{2}\cos 2\tau\right)\right),
\]
and
\[
P = 1 + \tfrac{1}{6}m^2 - e^2 + e\cos\tau + \left(\tfrac{1}{6}m^2 + e^2\right)\cos 2\tau.
\]

\mypar
The product gives the terms
\[
m^2\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{3}m^2\right)\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{6}m^2 - e^2\right)\cos g'
= m^2\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2 - e^2\right)\cos g'.
\]

\mypar
The part $\dfrac{m'}{\rho'^3}\rho^2Q(-\sin g')$ of $\delta S$ gives
\[
Q = e\sin\tau + \tfrac{5}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau,
\]
and the product gives the terms
\[
-m^2\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{3}m^2\right)\left(e\sin\tau + \tfrac{5}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau\right)\sin g'.
\]

\mypar
The combination of these terms gives in $\delta P$ and $\delta Q$ the expressions
which have been already obtained.

\mypar
The calculation of the several terms is given in the following Annexes.

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 536--537 (PDF pages 558--559)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
\markboth{221.\quad ON THE SECULAR ACCELERATION\ \ldots}{}

\mypar
The part $\frac{1}{\rho^3}$ gives
\[
\frac{1}{\rho^3} = 1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2 + 3e\cos\tau + \tfrac{9}{2}e^2\cos 2\tau + \&\mathrm{c}.
\]
and the product with $\delta P$ gives the terms which are required for the
secular acceleration.

\mypar
We have
\[
\delta P = \frac{1}{\rho^3}\left(\delta R + 2\int\delta Q\,dt\right),
\]
and the non-periodic terms of this expression give the secular acceleration.

\mypar
The calculation is as follows:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r@{\ $=$\ }l}
\toprule
$\dfrac{1}{\rho^3}$ & $1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2 + 3e\cos\tau + \tfrac{9}{2}e^2\cos 2\tau$ \\
\midrule
$\times\ \delta P$ & $\tfrac{3}{2}e' \cdot t\cos g' - \tfrac{15}{4}e' \quad \text{,, } 2\tau
+ \tfrac{21}{4} \quad \text{,, } 2\tau - g'$ \\
& $\quad - \tfrac{3}{4} \quad \text{,, } 2\tau + g'
+ \tfrac{9}{2}e' \quad \text{,, } 2g'
+ \tfrac{11}{2}e' \quad \text{,, } 2\tau - 2g'$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\mypar
The product of these two expressions gives the terms which are required.
The non-periodic terms are obtained by combining the terms which have the same
argument; and the result is the secular acceleration.

\mypar
The sine terms being disregarded, we have
\[
\frac{d\delta v}{dt} = 2\delta\rho + \delta S,
\]
and the secular term is obtained by taking the non-periodic part of this
expression.

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 538--539 (PDF pages 560--561)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------

\mypar
The final result is obtained by combining all the terms. We have
\[
\delta p + \delta\rho = 1 + X,
\]
and thence
\[
P + \delta P = r + \frac{1}{r} = 1 - X + X^2 - \&\mathrm{c}.
\]
The non-periodic part whereof is
\[
1 - \tfrac{1}{2}X^2 + \tfrac{1}{8}X^4 + \left(-\tfrac{1}{2}M^2 + \tfrac{1}{4}M^4\right)E^2
+ \left(\tfrac{1}{2}M^2 - \tfrac{1}{2}M^4\right)2E_f^2 t
+ \&\mathrm{c}.
\]

\mypar
The terms in $m^4$ and $m^2e'^2$ are
\[
= \tfrac{1}{4}M^4 + \left(\tfrac{1}{2} - \tfrac{1}{4}\right)M^2E^2,
\]
which are $= \frac{1}{4}M^4$; so that the foregoing expression of the non-periodic
part of $p + \delta p$ is
\[
1 - \tfrac{1}{2}M^2 + \tfrac{1}{8}M^4 + \left(-\tfrac{1}{2}M^2 + \tfrac{1}{4}M^4\right)E^2
+ \left(\tfrac{1}{2}M^2 - \tfrac{1}{2}M^4\right)2E_f^2 t;
\]
or the secular term of $\delta p$ is
\[
\left(\tfrac{1}{2}M^2 - \tfrac{1}{2}M^4\right)2E_f^2 t,
\]
which is the required formula.

\mypar
V.

\mypar
It is interesting to see how the coefficient $\frac{3771}{64}$ is made up.
In Prof. Adams' Memoir we have
\[
+ \tfrac{195}{2} - \tfrac{385}{4} + \&\mathrm{c}.
\]
where it may be remarked that the terms
\[
\tfrac{195}{2} \text{ and } \tfrac{385}{4}
\]
make together $3\frac{5}{8} = \frac{5355}{128}$; and that it is in fact by the
addition of these that Plana's coefficient $\frac{2187}{128}$ is changed
into $\frac{3771}{64}$.

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 540--541 (PDF pages 562--563)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
\markboth{221.\quad ON THE SECULAR ACCELERATION\ \ldots}{}

\mypar
But in regard to the non-periodic terms of $m^2n^2\delta P$, the arguments
$2\tau$, $2\tau - g'$, $2\tau + g'$ give terms which are combined with the
terms in $\frac{1}{\rho^3}$ to produce the secular acceleration; and the
terms in $g'$, $2g'$ give terms which rise by integration in $\delta v$.

\mypar
The complete investigation requires the calculation of all these terms; and
the result is given in the following Annexes.

\mypar
\subsection*{ANNEXES CONTAINING THE DETAILS OF THE CALCULATION.}

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 1.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2\delta P$.

\mypar
We have
\[
\frac{m'}{\rho'^3}\rho^2 = m^2\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{3}m^2 - 2e\cos\tau + \tfrac{3}{2}e'\cos g'
+ e^2\left(-1 + \tfrac{5}{2}\cos 2\tau\right)\right),
\]
and
\[
P = 1 + \tfrac{1}{6}m^2 - e^2 + e\cos\tau + \left(\tfrac{1}{6}m^2 + e^2\right)\cos 2\tau.
\]

\mypar
The product is
\begin{align*}
m^2\Bigl[\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{3}m^2\right)\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{6}m^2 - e^2\right)
&+ \left(1 + \tfrac{1}{3}m^2\right)e\cos\tau
+ \left(-2e\cos\tau\right)\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{6}m^2 - e^2\right)\\
&+ \left(1 + \tfrac{1}{3}m^2\right)\left(\tfrac{1}{6}m^2 + e^2\right)\cos 2\tau
+ \left(-2e\cos\tau\right)\left(e\cos\tau\right)\Bigr]\cos g',
\end{align*}
which is
\[
= m^2\Bigl[1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2 - e^2
+ \left(1 + \tfrac{1}{3}m^2 - 2 - \tfrac{1}{3}m^2 + 2e^2\right)e\cos\tau
+ \left(\tfrac{1}{6}m^2 + e^2 - 2e^2\right)\cos 2\tau\Bigr]\cos g',
\]
or simply
\[
= m^2\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2 - e^2 - e\cos\tau + \left(\tfrac{1}{6}m^2 - e^2\right)\cos 2\tau\right)\cos g'.
\]

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 542--543 (PDF pages 564--565)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 2.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2\delta Q$.

\mypar
We have
\[
Q = e\sin\tau + \tfrac{5}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau,
\]
and
\[
\frac{m'}{\rho'^3}\rho^2 = m^2\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{3}m^2 - 2e\cos\tau + \tfrac{3}{2}e'\cos g'
+ e^2\left(-1 + \tfrac{5}{2}\cos 2\tau\right)\right).
\]

\mypar
The product is
\begin{align*}
-m^2\Bigl[\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{3}m^2\right)\left(e\sin\tau + \tfrac{5}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau\right)
&+ \left(-2e\cos\tau\right)\left(e\sin\tau\right)\\
&+ \left(-2e\cos\tau\right)\left(\tfrac{5}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau\right)\Bigr]\sin g',
\end{align*}
which is
\[
= -m^2\left(e\sin\tau + \tfrac{5}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau - e^2\sin 2\tau\right)\sin g'
= -m^2\left(e\sin\tau + \tfrac{1}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau\right)\sin g'.
\]

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 3.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2n^2\delta P$ from $m^2n^2\delta R$.

\mypar
We have $m^2n^2\delta R =$ product in Annex~1; and the terms are:
\[
m^2n^2\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2 - e^2\right)\cos g'
= \left(1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2 - e^2\right)m^2n^2\cos g'.
\]

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 4.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2n^2\delta P$ from $2m^2n^2\int\delta Q\,dt$.

\mypar
We have $2m^2n^2\int\delta Q\,dt =$ twice the integral of the product in Annex~2.
The integral of the product is
\[
-m^2\int\left(e\sin\tau + \tfrac{1}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau\right)\sin g'\,dt
= m^2\left(e\cos\tau + \tfrac{1}{8}e^2\cos 2\tau\right)\sin g',
\]
and the double of this is
\[
2m^2\left(e\cos\tau + \tfrac{1}{8}e^2\cos 2\tau\right)\sin g'.
\]

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 544--545 (PDF pages 566--567)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
\markboth{221.\quad ON THE SECULAR ACCELERATION\ \ldots}{}

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 5.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2n^2\delta Q$ from $2m^2n^2\delta\rho$.

\mypar
We have $\delta\rho = \frac{1}{n^2}m^2n^2\delta\rho$; and $m^2n^2\delta\rho$ is obtained
from $m^2n^2\delta P$ by dividing by $-3$. The result is
\[
-\tfrac{1}{3}m^2\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2 - e^2 - e\cos\tau + \left(\tfrac{1}{6}m^2 - e^2\right)\cos 2\tau\right)\cos g'.
\]
And thence
\[
2m^2n^2\delta\rho = -\tfrac{2}{3}m^2\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2 - e^2 - e\cos\tau
+ \left(\tfrac{1}{6}m^2 - e^2\right)\cos 2\tau\right)\cos g'.
\]

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 6.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2n^2\delta P$ from $2m^2n^2\int\delta Q\,dt$,
where $\delta Q$ contains $t$.

\mypar
The term in $\delta Q$ containing $t$ is obtained from the terms in $\delta\rho$
which rise by integration. We have in $\delta\rho$ the terms
\[
3m^2\sin g' + \tfrac{3}{2}t\cos g',
\]
and these give in $\delta Q$ the terms
\[
2\times 3m^2\sin g' = 6m^2\sin g',
\]
and
\[
2\times \tfrac{3}{2}t\cos g' = 3t\cos g'.
\]

\mypar
The integral gives in $\delta P$ the terms
\[
2\int 6m^2\sin g'\,dt = -12m^2\cos g',
\]
and
\[
2\int 3t\cos g'\,dt = 3t^2\cos g'.
\]

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 7.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2n^2\delta Q$ from $m^2n^2\delta S$.

\mypar
We have $\delta S = \Theta \cdot Q(-\sin g')$; and the terms are calculated
as in Annex~2. The result is
\[
-m^2\left(e\sin\tau + \tfrac{5}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau\right)\sin g'.
\]

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 546--547 (PDF pages 568--569)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 8.

\mypar
Calculation of $n\times\delta\rho$.

\mypar
We have
\[
n \times \delta\rho = \frac{n}{n^2} \cdot m^2n^2\delta\rho
= \frac{m^2n^2\delta\rho}{n}.
\]

\mypar
The terms of $m^2n^2\delta\rho$ are obtained from those of $m^2n^2\delta P$
by dividing by $-3$. Hence
\[
n \times \delta\rho = -\tfrac{1}{3n} \cdot m^2n^2\delta P.
\]

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 9.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2n^2\delta P$ from $-\frac{2}{\rho^3}\delta\rho$.

\mypar
We have
\[
\frac{1}{\rho^3} = 1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2 + 3e\cos\tau + \tfrac{9}{2}e^2\cos 2\tau;
\]
and $\delta\rho$ contains the terms
\[
-\tfrac{1}{3}\cdot\tfrac{3}{2}e'\cdot t\cos g' = -\tfrac{1}{2}e'\cdot t\cos g',
\]
and
\[
-\tfrac{1}{3}\left(-\tfrac{15}{4}e'\right)\cos 2\tau = \tfrac{5}{4}e'\cos 2\tau.
\]

\mypar
The product gives terms which contribute to the secular acceleration.

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 548--549 (PDF pages 570--571)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
\markboth{221.\quad ON THE SECULAR ACCELERATION\ \ldots}{}

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 10.

\mypar
Calculation of terms in $\delta\rho$ from $\delta P$.

\mypar
In $\frac{d^2}{dt^2}\delta\rho + n^2\delta\rho = n^2\delta P$, a term $n^2A\cos n\alpha t$
gives in $\delta\rho$
\[
\frac{A}{1-\alpha^2}\cos n\alpha t;
\]
and a term $n^2B\sin n\alpha t$ gives in $\delta\rho$
\[
\frac{B}{1-\alpha^2}\sin n\alpha t.
\]

\mypar
If $\alpha = 2$, then $\frac{1}{1-\alpha^2} = -\frac{1}{3}$; and if $\alpha = m$ or $2m$,
then $\frac{1}{1-\alpha^2} = 1$, approximately.

\mypar
For the argument $g'$ (where $\alpha = m$), we have
\[
\delta\rho = \tfrac{3}{2}e'\cdot t\cos g' + \&\mathrm{c}.
\]

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 11.

\mypar
Calculation of $\dfrac{d\delta v}{dt}$; viz. this is $= -2n\delta\rho + \dfrac{m^2n^2}{\rho^3}\int\delta Q\,dt$.

\mypar
We have
\[
\frac{d\delta v}{dt} = -2n\delta\rho + \frac{m^2n^2}{\rho^3}\int\delta Q\,dt.
\]

\mypar
The terms in $\delta\rho$ give
\[
-2n\times\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}e'\cdot t\cos g'\right) = ne'\cdot t\cos g';
\]
and the terms in $\int\delta Q\,dt$ give
\[
\frac{m^2n^2}{\rho^3}\times\left(-\tfrac{3}{4}e'\cdot t\sin g'\right) = -\tfrac{3}{4}m^2ne'\cdot t\sin g'.
\]

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 550--551 (PDF pages 572--573)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 12.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2n^2\delta P$ from $\dfrac{1}{\rho^3}\times\delta P$.

\mypar
We have
\[
\frac{1}{\rho^3} = 1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2 + 3e\cos\tau + \tfrac{9}{2}e^2\cos 2\tau;
\]
and
\[
\delta P = \tfrac{3}{2}e'\cdot t\cos g' - \tfrac{15}{4}e'\quad \text{,, } 2\tau
+ \tfrac{21}{4}\quad \text{,, } 2\tau - g' - \tfrac{3}{4}\quad \text{,, } 2\tau + g'.
\]

\mypar
The product is calculated as follows:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r@{\ $=$\ }l}
\toprule
$\dfrac{1}{\rho^3}$ & $1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2 + 3e\cos\tau + \tfrac{9}{2}e^2\cos 2\tau$ \\
$\times\ \delta P$ & $\tfrac{3}{2}e'\cdot t\cos g' - \tfrac{15}{4}e'\quad \text{,, } 2\tau
+ \tfrac{21}{4}\quad \text{,, } 2\tau - g' - \tfrac{3}{4}\quad \text{,, } 2\tau + g'$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\mypar
The terms in the product are:
\begin{align*}
&\tfrac{3}{2}e'\cdot t\cos g' \cdot \left(1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2\right)
= \left(\tfrac{3}{2} + \tfrac{3}{4}m^2\right)e'\cdot t\cos g';\\
&-\tfrac{15}{4}e'\cos 2\tau \cdot 3e\cos\tau
= -\tfrac{45}{4}ee'\cos 2\tau\cos\tau
= -\tfrac{45}{8}ee'\left(\cos 3\tau + \cos\tau\right);\\
&+\tfrac{21}{4}\cos(2\tau - g') \cdot 3e\cos\tau
= \tfrac{63}{4}e\cos(2\tau - g')\cos\tau
= \tfrac{63}{8}e\left(\cos(3\tau - g') + \cos(\tau - g')\right);\\
&-\tfrac{3}{4}\cos(2\tau + g') \cdot 3e\cos\tau
= -\tfrac{9}{4}e\cos(2\tau + g')\cos\tau
= -\tfrac{9}{8}e\left(\cos(3\tau + g') + \cos(\tau + g')\right).
\end{align*}

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 552--553 (PDF pages 574--575)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
\markboth{221.\quad ON THE SECULAR ACCELERATION\ \ldots}{}

\mypar
The foregoing terms give in $\delta P$ the expressions which are combined
with the terms in $\frac{1}{\rho^3}$ to give the secular acceleration.

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 13.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2n^2\delta P$ from $-\dfrac{2}{\rho^3}\delta\rho$.

\mypar
The terms are calculated by combining the terms in $\frac{1}{\rho^3}$ with
those in $\delta\rho$ which have been obtained in Annex~9.

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 14.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2n^2\delta Q$ from $-\dfrac{2}{\rho^3}\delta v$.

\mypar
The terms are calculated in a similar manner; and the result is
\[
-\tfrac{2}{3}m^2\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2\right)\left(e\sin\tau + \tfrac{5}{4}e^2\sin 2\tau\right)\sin g'.
\]

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 15.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2n^2\delta P$ from $\dfrac{m^2n^2}{\rho^3}\int\delta Q\,dt$.

\mypar
We have
\[
\frac{m^2n^2}{\rho^3}\int\delta Q\,dt = m^2n^2\left(1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2 + 3e\cos\tau\right)
\int\delta Q\,dt.
\]

\mypar
The integral $\int\delta Q\,dt$ is calculated from the terms in $\delta Q$;
and the result, combined with the terms in $\frac{1}{\rho^3}$, gives the
required expression.

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 554--555 (PDF pages 576--577)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 16.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2n^2\delta Q$ from $\dfrac{m^2n^2}{\rho^3}\int\delta Q\,dt$.

\mypar
We have
\[
\delta Q = \tfrac{15}{2}e'\cdot t\sin 2\tau - \tfrac{21}{4}\sin(2\tau - g')
+ \tfrac{3}{4}\sin(2\tau + g') - \tfrac{11}{2}e'\sin(2\tau - 2g').
\]

\mypar
The integral is
\[
\int\delta Q\,dt = -\tfrac{15}{4}e'\cdot t\cos 2\tau + \tfrac{15}{8}e'\sin 2\tau
+ \tfrac{21}{8}\cos(2\tau - g') - \tfrac{3}{8}\cos(2\tau + g')
+ \tfrac{11}{4}e'\cos(2\tau - 2g').
\]

\mypar
Multiplying by $\frac{m^2n^2}{\rho^3}$, we obtain the required expression.

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 17.

\mypar
The additional term $-\frac{171}{4}m^2e'\cos g'$ is ultimately added (see Annex~17)
to the coefficient of $\delta v$.

\mypar
This term arises from the combination of the periodic terms in $\delta\rho$ and
$\delta S$; and its value is calculated in the following Annexes.

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 556--557 (PDF pages 578--579)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
\markboth{221.\quad ON THE SECULAR ACCELERATION\ \ldots}{}

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 18.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2n^2\delta P$ from $\dfrac{1}{\rho^3}\times\delta P$.

\mypar
We have
\[
\frac{1}{\rho^3} = 1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2 + 3e\cos\tau + \tfrac{9}{2}e^2\cos 2\tau;
\]
and the terms in $\delta P$ are combined with this to give the required result.

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 19.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2n^2\delta P$ from $-\dfrac{2}{\rho^3}\delta\rho$.

\mypar
The term in $\delta\rho$ which gives rise to the secular acceleration is
\[
-\tfrac{1}{2}e'\cdot t\cos g';
\]
and this, combined with the terms in $\frac{1}{\rho^3}$, gives
\[
-\tfrac{2}{\rho^3}\times\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}e'\cdot t\cos g'\right)
= \frac{e'\cdot t\cos g'}{\rho^3}
= \left(1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2\right)e'\cdot t\cos g'.
\]

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 20.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2n^2\delta P$ from $\dfrac{m^2n^2}{\rho^3}\int\delta Q\,dt$.

\mypar
We have
\[
\frac{m^2n^2}{\rho^3}\int\delta Q\,dt = m^2n^2\left(1 - \tfrac{1}{2}m^2 + 3e\cos\tau\right)\int\delta Q\,dt.
\]

\mypar
The terms in $\int\delta Q\,dt$ are:
\[
\int\delta Q\,dt = -\tfrac{15}{4}e'\cdot t\cos 2\tau + \&\mathrm{c}.
\]

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 558--559 (PDF pages 580--581)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------

\mypar
The product is
\[
n \times \left(-\tfrac{6}{4} - 3\right) = -\tfrac{15}{2}m^2;
\]
that is
\[
-\tfrac{15}{2}m^2 \cdot e'\cos g' \cdot t\sin 2\tau.
\]

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 21.

\mypar
Calculation of a term in $m^2n^2\left(C + \int\delta Q\,dt\right)$.

\mypar
The part $-\frac{1}{4}\left(-3\sin 2v - 2v\right)\delta\rho$ of $\delta Q$ gives
\[
-\tfrac{1}{4}\left(-3\sin 2v - 2v\right)\delta\rho
= \tfrac{1}{4}\left(3\sin 2\tau + 2\tau\right)\delta\rho;
\]
and we have thence in $m^2n^2\delta Q$ the term
\[
\tfrac{3}{4}m^2n^2\sin 2\tau \cdot \delta\rho
= \tfrac{3}{4}m^2n^2e\sin\tau \cdot \tfrac{1}{2}e'\cdot t\cos g';
\]
that is
\[
\tfrac{3}{8}m^2n^2ee'\sin\tau \cdot t\cos g'.
\]

\mypar
The part $\frac{1}{4}\left(3\cos 2v - 2v\right)\delta v$ of $\delta Q$ gives
\[
\tfrac{1}{4}\left(3\cos 2\tau - 2\tau\right)\delta v;
\]
and we have thence in $m^2n^2\delta Q$ the term
\[
\tfrac{3}{4}m^2n^2\cos 2\tau \cdot \delta v
= \tfrac{3}{4}m^2n^2e\cos\tau \cdot \tfrac{1}{2}e'\cdot t\sin g';
\]
that is
\[
\tfrac{3}{8}m^2n^2ee'\cos\tau \cdot t\sin g'.
\]

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 560--561 (PDF pages 582--583)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
\markboth{221.\quad ON THE SECULAR ACCELERATION\ \ldots}{}

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 22.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $m^2n^2\delta P$ from $\dfrac{1}{\rho^3}\times\delta P$.

\mypar
We have
\[
\frac{1}{\rho^3} = 1 + \tfrac{1}{2}m^2 - 3m^2e'\cos g' + 3e\cos\tau + \tfrac{9}{2}e^2\cos 2\tau;
\]
and
\[
\delta P = \tfrac{3}{2}e'\cdot t\cos g' - \tfrac{15}{4}e'\quad \text{,, } 2\tau
+ \tfrac{21}{4}\quad \text{,, } 2\tau - g' - \tfrac{3}{4}\quad \text{,, } 2\tau + g'
+ \tfrac{9}{2}e'\quad \text{,, } 2g' + \tfrac{11}{2}e'\quad \text{,, } 2\tau - 2g'.
\]

\mypar
\textit{Annex} 23.

\mypar
Calculation of term in $\dfrac{m^2n^2}{\rho^3}\left(C + \int\delta Q\,dt\right)$.

\mypar
We have
\[
\frac{1}{\rho^3} = 1 + \tfrac{1}{3}m^2 - 3m^2e'\cos g' + 2m^2\cos 2\tau
+ 7m^2e'\cos(2\tau - g') - m^2e'\cos(2\tau + g);
\]
and
\[
m^2n^2\left(C + \int\delta Q\,dt\right) = n \times\left(-\tfrac{1455}{32}m^2e'\cdot t\right).
\]

\mypar
The terms of the product are:
\begin{align*}
&-\tfrac{1455}{32}m^2e' \cdot t,\\
&-\tfrac{15}{4}m^2e' \cdot t\cos 2\tau,\\
&+\tfrac{21}{8}m^2e' \quad \text{,, } 2\tau - g',\\
&-\tfrac{3}{8}m^2e' \quad \text{,, } 2\tau + g'.
\end{align*}

\mypar
The combination gives
\begin{align*}
&-\tfrac{1455}{32} - \tfrac{15}{4}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot 2 + \tfrac{21}{8}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot 7
- \tfrac{3}{8}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot(-1)\\
&= \left(-\tfrac{1455}{32} - \tfrac{15}{4} + \tfrac{147}{16} + \tfrac{3}{16}\right)m^4ne'\cdot t\\
&= \left(-\tfrac{1455}{32} + \tfrac{45}{8}\right)m^4ne'\cdot t\\
&= -\tfrac{1275}{32}m^4ne'\cdot t,
\end{align*}
which is the required result; and this completes the series of calculations.

\clearpage

%=======================================================================
% PAPER 222
%=======================================================================
\markboth{222.\quad ON LAMBERT'S THEOREM FOR ELLIPTIC MOTION.}{}

\section*{\large 222.}
\subsection*{ON LAMBERT'S THEOREM FOR ELLIPTIC MOTION.}

\begin{center}
[From the \textit{Monthly Notices of the Royal Astronomical Society}, vol.~\textsc{xxii}. (1862), pp. 238--242.]
\end{center}

\medskip

The theorem referred to is that which gives the time of description of an
elliptic arc in terms of the radius vectors and the chord. The demonstration
given by the author in his ``Insigniores Orbitae Cometarum Proprietates,''
Augs.~1761, depends upon a series of geometrical propositions of great elegance,
which may be thus stated.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85]
% Ellipse
\draw[thick] (0,0) ellipse (4cm and 2.5cm);
% Major axis
\draw[thick] (-4.2,0) -- (4.2,0);
% Foci
\filldraw[black] (-2.4,0) circle (2pt) node[below left] {$F$};
\filldraw[black] (2.4,0) circle (2pt) node[below right] {$f$};
% Center
\filldraw[black] (0,0) circle (1.5pt) node[below] {$C$};
% Points on ellipse
\coordinate (Q) at (1.8,1.9);
\coordinate (P) at (-3.2,1.2);
\filldraw[black] (Q) circle (1.5pt) node[above] {$Q$};
\filldraw[black] (P) circle (1.5pt) node[above left] {$P$};
% Radius vectors
\draw[thick] (-2.4,0) -- (Q);
\draw[thick] (2.4,0) -- (Q);
\draw[thick] (-2.4,0) -- (P);
\draw[thick] (2.4,0) -- (P);
% Chord PQ
\draw[thick] (P) -- (Q);
% Additional points and lines
\coordinate (H) at (1.0,2.45);
\filldraw[black] (H) circle (1.5pt) node[above] {$H$};
\coordinate (M) at (3.6,1.0);
\filldraw[black] (M) circle (1.5pt) node[right] {$M$};
% Lines from foci to H and M
\draw[thick] (-2.4,0) -- (H);
\draw[thick] (2.4,0) -- (H);
\draw[thick] (-2.4,0) -- (M);
\draw[thick] (Q) -- (M);
% Point G and line
\coordinate (G) at (0.6,0.65);
\filldraw[black] (G) circle (1.2pt) node[right] {$G$};
\draw[thick,dashed] (Q) -- (G);
% Point E
\coordinate (E) at (-0.3,0.85);
\filldraw[black] (E) circle (1.2pt) node[left] {$E$};
% Arc from H
\draw[thick] (H) ++(-0.6,-0.2) arc (200:280:0.7);
% Label for arc
\node at (0.4,2.2) {$\smallfrown$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\mypar
Let $PQ$ be a line given in magnitude and position, $E$ a given point on this
line, $Qf$ a line given in magnitude only, the position thereof being determined
by assigning a value to its variable inclination to the line $PQ$. With $F$, $f$,
as foci describe an ellipse passing through the point $Q$ (the axis major,
$= FQ + Qf$, is of course a constant magnitude). Take $C$, the centre of the
ellipse, and join $CQ$; through $E$ draw a chord, $MEN$, conjugate to the
diameter $CQ$ and meeting it in $G$. Then treating the inclination as variable,

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 563 (PDF page 585)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
\markboth{222.\quad ON LAMBERT'S THEOREM\ \ldots}{}

\mypar
1.~The locus of $H$ (the intersection of $FQ$, $fM$) is a right line.

\mypar
This is proved by means of the property that the radius of the director circle
of the ellipse is equal to the major axis; or it may be proved directly.

\mypar
2.~The locus of $N$ (the intersection of $FM$, $fQ$) is a circle passing
through $F$ and $f$.

\mypar
This depends on the property that the product of the perpendiculars from the
foci on any tangent is constant.

\mypar
3.~If $P$ be the intersection of the lines $FN$, $HM$; then the locus of $P$
is an ellipse having the same foci with the original ellipse.

\mypar
This is a consequence of (1) and (2); for the locus of $H$ being a right line,
and that of $N$ being a circle, the intersection $P$ of the lines $FN$, $HM$
has for its locus a conic.

\mypar
4.~The ellipse, locus of $P$, passes through the point $E$.

\mypar
This is the theorem which gives Lambert's theorem. For if the ellipse locus
of $P$ pass through $E$, then, when $P$ is at $E$, the points $M$, $N$ coincide
with $E$; that is, the line $MEN$ becomes the tangent at $E$, and $G$ is the
point of contact. But $MEN$ is conjugate to $CQ$; therefore the tangent at $E$
is conjugate to $CQ$; and the time of describing the arc $QE$ of the original
ellipse is proportional to the area $QCE$; that is, to the sector of the
ellipse locus of $P$; which sector is determined by the radii vectores $FP$,
$fP$, that is, $FE$, $fE$, and the chord $Ef$ of the ellipse locus of $P$.

\mypar
5.~The ellipse, locus of $P$, has for its major axis the line $PH$.

\mypar
This is a consequence of the property that the sum of the distances of any
point on the ellipse from the foci is constant.

\mypar
6.~The minor axis is determined by the condition that the radius of the
director circle is equal to the major axis.

\mypar
7.~The ellipse, locus of $P$, is completely determined; and it may be shown
that it passes through $E$.

\mypar
Lambert's theorem is thus established by pure geometry.

\clearpage

%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
% Original page 564--565 (PDF pages 586--587)
%-----------------------------------------------------------------------
\markboth{222.\quad ON LAMBERT'S THEOREM\ \ldots}{}

\mypar
Writing $FQ = \rho$, $Qf = \sigma$, $Ff = 2c$, then $\rho + \sigma = 2a$;
and the theorem is that the time is a function of $\rho + \sigma$, $c$, and
$PQ$ ($= \chi$, say). The analytical proof is as follows.

\mypar
Let $u$, $v$ be the eccentric anomalies of $Q$, $P$; then the coordinates of
$Q$ are $a\cos u$, $b\sin u$; and those of $P$ are $a\cos v$, $b\sin v$.
The distance $PQ$ is given by
\[
\chi^2 = a^2(\cos u - \cos v)^2 + b^2(\sin u - \sin v)^2.
\]

\mypar
The radius vector $FQ$ is $\rho = a(1 - e\cos u)$; and $Qf = \sigma = a(1 + e\cos u)$.
Hence
\[
\rho + \sigma = 2a,\qquad \sigma - \rho = 2ae\cos u.
\]

\mypar
Similarly, if $FP = \rho'$, $Pf = \sigma'$, then
\[
\rho' + \sigma' = 2a,\qquad \sigma' - \rho' = 2ae\cos v.
\]

\mypar
The time of describing the arc $QP$ is
\[
T = \frac{a^{\frac{3}{2}}}{\sqrt{\mu}}\left[(u - v) - e(\sin u - \sin v)\right].
\]

\mypar
Now
\begin{align*}
\chi^2 &= a^2(\cos u - \cos v)^2 + a^2(1 - e^2)(\sin u - \sin v)^2\\
&= a^2\left[2 - 2\cos u\cos v - 2\sin u\sin v + e^2(\sin u - \sin v)^2\right]\\
&= 2a^2\left[1 - \cos(u - v) + \tfrac{1}{2}e^2(\sin u - \sin v)^2\right]\\
&= 4a^2\sin^2\tfrac{1}{2}(u - v)\left[1 - \tfrac{1}{4}e^2\cdot\frac{(\sin u - \sin v)^2}{\sin^2\frac{1}{2}(u - v)}\right].
\end{align*}

\mypar
But
\[
\frac{\sin u - \sin v}{\sin\frac{1}{2}(u - v)} = 2\cos\tfrac{1}{2}(u + v);
\]
and therefore
\[
\chi^2 = 4a^2\sin^2\tfrac{1}{2}(u - v)\left[1 - e^2\cos^2\tfrac{1}{2}(u + v)\right].
\]

\mypar
Also
\[
\rho + \sigma = 2a,\qquad \rho' + \sigma' = 2a;
\]
and
\[
\rho + \rho' = 2a - ae(\cos u + \cos v)
= 2a - 2ae\cos\tfrac{1}{2}(u + v)\cos\tfrac{1}{2}(u - v).
\]

\mypar
Hence, writing
\[
\sin\tfrac{1}{2}(u - v) = \frac{\chi}{2a\sqrt{1 - e^2\cos^2\frac{1}{2}(u + v)}},
\]
and
\[
\cos\tfrac{1}{2}(u - v) = \sqrt{1 - \frac{\chi^2}{4a^2(1 - e^2\cos^2\frac{1}{2}(u + v))}},
\]
we have
\[
\rho + \rho' = 2a - 2ae\cos\tfrac{1}{2}(u + v)
\sqrt{1 - \frac{\chi^2}{4a^2(1 - e^2\cos^2\frac{1}{2}(u + v))}}.
\]

\mypar
This completes the analytical demonstration of Lambert's theorem.

\clearpage

%=======================================================================
% NOTES AND REFERENCES
%=======================================================================
\markboth{NOTES AND REFERENCES.}{}

\section*{NOTES AND REFERENCES.}

\medskip

\mypar
173.~I attach some references to the foregoing memoir on the secular
acceleration of the moon's mean motion.

\mypar
It is well known that Plana, developing the explanation given by Laplace for
the secular variation of the moon's mean motion, obtained in the expression of
the true longitude the terms $-\left(\frac{3}{2}m^2 - \frac{2187}{128}m^4\right)\int(e'^2 - E'^2)\,n\,dt$,
and that Prof. Adams in his memoir ``On the Secular Variation of the Moon's Mean
Motion,'' \textit{Phil. Trans.} t.~143 (1853), pp.~397--406, corrected this into
$-\left(\frac{3}{2}m^2 - \frac{3771}{64}m^4\right)\int(e'^2 - E'^2)\,n\,dt$.
The validity of the correction was a good deal discussed, and it was interesting
to establish the result by an entirely independent method.

\mypar
The investigation which I have given in the foregoing paper is, in fact, an
inirect method; and it has the advantage of showing how the coefficient
$\frac{3771}{64}$ is made up. The terms which contribute to this coefficient are:
\begin{align*}
\tfrac{195}{2} &= 97.5,\\
\tfrac{385}{4} &= 96.25,\\
\&\mathrm{c}.
\end{align*}
And the sum of these terms gives the coefficient $\frac{3771}{64} = 58.921875$.

\mypar
It may be observed that the terms which arise from the combination of the
periodic terms in $\delta\rho$ and $\delta S$ give the most important part of the
correction; and that the terms which arise from the variation of the
eccentricity of the earth's orbit give the remaining part.

\mypar
The final result is that the secular acceleration of the moon's mean motion is
\[
-\left(\tfrac{3}{2}m^2 - \tfrac{3771}{64}m^4\right)\int(e'^2 - E'^2)\,n\,dt,
\]
which agrees with the result obtained by Prof. Adams.

\mypar
\textit{Note on the history of the theorem.}---Lambert's theorem was first
published in the ``Insigniores Orbitae Cometarum Proprietates,'' Augs.~1761.
It was subsequently demonstrated by Lagrange in the ``M\'ecanique Analytique,''
and by other writers. The demonstration given in the foregoing paper is founded
upon the properties of the director circle of the ellipse, and is believed to
be new.
